# Change Log

## Unreleased

### Added

- **2026-11-18 — Diff can overlay the two structures: a Kabsch/Horn least-squares fit with its RMSD, a per-atom displacement ramp and a morph slider, chosen from a Display row every Diff panel now carries (version 1.0.5; the version number is not bumped)** — `media/viewer.js`, `media/viewer.css`.

  **The choice** — After Diff activates, the panel opens with a **Display** row: **Side by side** (the comparison exactly as it was) and **Overlay**. The row is drawn in the refusal panels too — a molecular skeleton mismatch, a cell mismatch, a not-comparable pair — with **Overlay** disabled and the reason in its tooltip, so the choice always lives where the comparison is. Switching to Overlay parks both halves' own rotation, pan and zoom and puts them back on the way out, so a look at the fit does not cost the side-by-side picture the user arranged.

  **The fit** — The overlay superposes the comparison side onto the structure on screen over the one-to-one mapping the skeleton match produced, using Horn's quaternion form of the Kabsch problem: the largest eigenvector of the 4×4 matrix built from the centred cross-covariance (found by a cyclic Jacobi solver) gives the rotation, and the translation puts the right centroid on the left one. The direction is explicit — with the covariance written left-against-right the eigenvector carries left→right, so the fit is applied through the conjugate quaternion. The panel reports the fit **RMSD**, the largest and the mean per-atom displacement, and the coordinates of neither structure are modified. The overlay draws both structures in the one viewport through one camera, the right at 0.62 of its sphere radius and 0.7 of its bond radius so coincident atoms and sticks do not swallow each other; the halves' labels become one `Overlay: … vs …`, and the status line carries the RMSD.

  **Displacement and morph** — **Colour atoms by displacement** paints every atom on both sides from its own fitted displacement on a blue → cyan → yellow → red ramp, with a legend from 0 Å to the largest displacement; while it is on it replaces the difference orange on atoms, and switching it off brings the orange back. The **Morph** slider slides the right structure from 0% — its own orientation, its centroid already on the left's — to 100%, the fitted pose; only the atom handles move, and the right-hand bond pieces are rebuilt from them, so sticks and balls always state the same geometry. Both controls exist only in overlay mode and stay hidden in the side-by-side panel. Orbit, pan (cursor-locked), wheel and pinch zoom, atom picking, box selection, auto-spin and PNG capture all run through the overlay's shared camera and the mapped atoms; an edit during the comparison refits, recolours and rewrites the panel's numbers instead of leaving stale ones, and a new comparison or leaving Diff clears the fit, the ramp and the morph.

  **Where it stops** — The overlay is offered only for a molecular comparison whose skeletons matched. A crystal comparison compares cells and stays side by side, an unmatched pair has no correspondence to fit through, and both disable **Overlay** with their reason on the button. Nothing else about the comparison changes: each half still rotates, pans and zooms independently in side-by-side mode, and the difference list, statistics, auto-mirror selection and crystal diff are untouched.

  Verified with `.workbuddy/tools/verify_diff_overlay.js` (**82 checks** in headless Edge: the **Display** row and its active/disabled states in the results, skeleton-mismatch and cell-mismatch panels, the fit's RMSD and its relation to the largest displacement, the morph row appearing only in overlay and the slider at 0% and 55%, the displacement toggle and legend, distinct PNG captures of the side-by-side, overlay and displacement pictures, orbit / pan / wheel zoom / box selection, leaving and re-entering the comparison, and no uncaught page error in any session), plus `verify_redo.js` (**94**), `verify_shortcuts.js` (**136**), `node --check media/viewer.js` and `npx tsc -p ./`.

- **2026-11-18 — Undo has a redo: a two-stack history behind Ctrl+Shift+Z, the toolbar's Redo button and the editor's own Redo command, carried across frames and molecules without losing the branch (version 1.0.5; the version number is not bumped)** — `media/viewer.js`, `src/webview/page.ts`, `src/webview/molecularViewer.ts`.

  **The second stack** — The page kept one pre-edit stack (`undoStack`, 50 entries) and the editor's Redo message was answered with nothing, so an undo could not be replayed. `redoStack` now sits beside it: an undo pushes the state it leaves onto the redo stack and a redo pushes it back onto the undo stack, so undo/redo walk the same edit chain in both directions; each stack is capped at 50 entries. A new edit after an undo starts a new branch — the old redo steps are dropped, as in any editor — but `pushUndo` parks them on the new snapshot (`snap.redoBefore`) and `dropUndoSnapshot()` puts them back when the gesture that took the snapshot never happened: a dialog's **Cancel**, a measurement dialog's **OK** when neither the geometry nor the bond changed, a drag released without moving, a bake that moved nothing, the Rotate Group slider returning to where it started. A snapshot restores everything an edit can touch — atoms, bonds and bond source, the crystal base cell, file table, bounds and disorder filter, the symmetry setting with its alternative, charge and multiplicity, and the provenance line — in both directions, so redo is not a lesser undo. The two functions return whether they moved; a no-op at either end of the chain neither rebuilds the scene nor claims a step. A measurement dialog's drop names the snapshot it pushed when it opened: an Import reply can land while the dialog is open and push an edit of its own on top, and the dialog's Cancel or untouched OK takes back only its own step, leaving the import's snapshot (and the branch it holds) as the real edit it is.

  **The history still belongs to the structure** — Frame and script-molecule switches park **both** stacks (`undoParked` / `redoParked`) under the same fixed atom budget (120 000 atoms over all parked histories, oldest out first) and adopt them back together, so leaving a frame after an undo and returning does not lose the redo; the budget charges a snapshot for the displaced redo branch it carries, so a parked history cannot hold twice what it is bounded to. The frame jumps that already carried the undo stack across — normal-mode playback, clicking an orbital's frame, choosing a charge scheme — now carry the redo stack with it in the same condition, and never from a different atom list. Rotate Group's **Reset** clears this session's tagged redo entries before dropping the tagged undo entries, so a redo branch that existed before the session and was restored by `dropUndoSnapshot()` is not mistaken for session state.

  **The ways in** — `<button id="redo-btn">` joins the toolbar beside Undo (disabled whenever the redo stack is empty), **Ctrl+Shift+Z / Cmd+Shift+Z** reaches it from the page (read from `key`/`code` so it works under a non-Latin layout, ignored while a text field or a dialog has focus, and not firing on Alt+Shift+Z), its tooltip names the platform's own modifier, and the editor's Redo command (`hostRedo`), previously ignored, now calls the same `doRedo()` as the button — the inverse of how `hostUndo` already worked. Both shortcuts act once per physical press: the page keeps its own press-and-release state for the key, so the first keydown steps and every keydown that arrives while the key is still down is consumed but steps nothing — whether or not the platform flags it as a repeat — and only the keyup (or losing focus) opens the door for the next step. Holding Ctrl+Z or Ctrl+Shift+Z therefore walks exactly one step per press-and-release instead of running down the whole history. Both host commands follow the page's own rule about an open dialog (a measurement dialog previews geometry on top of a snapshot of its own), so the editor's menus cannot step the history underneath a preview that the dialog's Cancel would then restore the wrong state over.

  Verified with `.workbuddy/tools/verify_redo.js` (**94 checks**: molecule and crystal round trips in both directions, a three-edit chain's order and the no-op return values, the branch parked on a post-undo edit and restored by Cancel, both stacks capped at 50, the parked budget charging a displaced branch, a dialog's drop refusing an edit that landed above its snapshot, the symmetry-setting switch, the Rotate Group angle with its leaving tag, the editor commands' dialog guard, the untouched measurement dialogs' OK dropping their own snapshot, and the host/button/keyboard wiring against the built page), `verify_shortcuts.js` (**136** — Win32, Mac, Cyrillic and AZERTY layouts, dialogs and text fields, the held-key latch stepping once per press and re-arming on keyup, and the redo tooltip), `verify_editor_channels.js` (**146** — including the redo stack's own parking and the atom budget counting both), `verify_crystal_edit.js` **159** (including an untouched dialog's OK giving back the redo branch its snapshot displaced), `verify_adp.js` **93**, `verify_crystal_addh.js` **58**, `verify_symmetry_check.js` **127**, `verify_provenance.js` **78**, `verify_crystal.js` at its pre-existing 250/255 (the five failures are the worktree's CRLF source pins and stale post-split needles, untouched here), headless Edge layout of the two-row toolbar with the extra button (`verify_toolbar.js`, **194**), `npm run compile` clean and the client's syntax check.

- **2026-11-17 — The viewer is an editable custom editor: it reports its edits, saves them through its own format dialog, reverts, and survives a window reload — and it says when the file changed under it** — `src/webview/molecularViewer.ts` implements `CustomEditorProvider` instead of `CustomReadonlyEditorProvider`. Every edit the page makes is reported once (`markEdited`, one message per snapshot that survives the gesture that took it), so the tab shows its modified dot and closing the tab or the window prompts instead of dropping the edits and their history silently. **Save** — `Ctrl+S`, *Save All*, or the Save button of the close prompt — writes through the page's own format dialog, which is the one place that knows what a format holds, and once this session has written a file it rewrites that same file in that same format without asking again; a dismissed dialog rejects with `CancellationError`, which is how the editor is told the document is still modified. **Save As** from the editor's own dialog is honoured by mapping the extension it chose to the content builder (`SAVE_AS_FORMATS`), so that file is what gets written. **Revert** re-reads the file, takes the result onto the document the panel already holds (`setContent`) and rebuilds the page. **Hot exit is real**: `backupCustomDocument` asks the page for its session — the structure on screen with its ghosts, bond orders, occupancy and electronic state, a crystal's base cell and bounds, and the trajectory frames edited earlier in the session within a 500 000-atom budget — writes it beside VS Code's own backups, and `openCustomDocument` hands it back so the page applies it (`applyRestoreSession`) once every panel and frame is in place; a session that does not validate leaves the file's own view, with the reason on the info panel. The editor's **Undo** command is routed into the page's one history (`hostUndo`), so it works while the webview does not have focus; Redo is deliberately answered with nothing, because the viewer keeps one stack and offering a second one would be a lie.

  **And the file on disk** — a watcher on the opened file tells the page when another program — an editor, a script, the job still appending to its own log — rewrote it. It never reloads on its own (an optimisation log changes every few seconds and a prompt per change is noise): it shows one notice at the top of the canvas, in the page's own markup (`#file-notice`, `display:none` until it has something to say, so a page whose file has not changed is laid out exactly as before), whose **Reload** asks the host to read the file again and rebuild the view; a deleted file says so and offers no reload. The panel's own writes are excluded by a two-second window, so saving onto the watched file is not reported back as an outside change.

  **The save identity** — the page now remembers the path and format it last wrote (`savePath` / `saveFormat`, separate from `MD.filePath`, which stays the file that was *opened* and is what the provenance line names). The host reports what it actually wrote (`saveResult`), so a dismissed dialog leaves the identity where it was and the next Save As starts beside the file just written instead of beside the file opened. A data export — the powder pattern's two-column text — travels as its own `saveDataFile` message, so it can never become the structure's save target (a later Save would otherwise write the structure into the `.xy` file).

  Verified with `.workbuddy/tools/verify_editor_channels.js` (**138 checks**: the summary's SCF stamps, the orbital drawability per spin channel, what a session stores and what it refuses, the parked undo history and its atom budget, the import's electronic state, the save/session/undo/notice wiring, the notice's own markup and style, and the provider's editor-facing contract — a save that wrote nothing rejects with `CancellationError`, Save As builds the format its destination implies, and a backup lands where VS Code suggested it), plus the revived suites — `verify_load_progress` **39**, `verify_shortcuts` **117** (`Ctrl+S` now asks for the editor's save), `verify_provenance` **78**, `verify_cif` **276**, `verify_crystal` **254**, `verify_crystal_edit` **155**, `verify_crystal_addh` **58**, `verify_adp` **92**, `probe_pdb_save`, and `npm run compile` clean.

- **2026-11-13 — Choosing the Orbitals tab takes the view to the geometry whose SCF the levels were computed at** — The level diagram draws the last SCF the file printed: the converged step of an optimisation, the last point of a relaxed scan, one point of an IRC path, or one job's geometry in a `--Link1--` file — not necessarily the frame the page opened on. `src/parsers/logParser.ts` now records, for each eigenvalue dump, the frame whose SCF printed it (`OrbitalDump.frame`); the last dump's index travels as `OrbitalLevels.frame` through the same pre-filter → `frameRemap` → final-list mapping a charge table uses, and through the IRC path reordering, so it names the frame the page actually holds. `src/webview/page.ts` forwards it, and `media/viewer.js` runs `orbitalJumpToFrame` when **Orbitals** is selected — or when a file whose only electronic structure is its orbitals opens straight on that tab. The jump is the move choosing a charge scheme already makes: the view goes to that frame and the undo history is carried across when both frames describe the same atom list, and it is a no-op when the SCF's frame is already on screen. The panel's hint follows the view: it reads `Eigenvalues of the SCF computed at this frame (107 of 107).` on that frame and `… at frame 107 of 107, not of the frame on screen.` after navigating away, instead of one generic last-SCF sentence; a checkpoint states no frame and keeps the old line.

  Verified in headless Edge (`.workbuddy/tools/verify_pathway_page.js`): the distance scan renders 107 frames, opens on the input pose, and clicking **Orbitals** moves to frame 107 (`Point 32 · step 3`) with the tab selected and the hint naming that frame; the IRC page renders its path in the same order. Parser checks in `verify_scan_irc.js` confirm the link on an optimisation's last frame, on the dihedral scan's point-19 frame (index 78), and on an IRC's frame 0.

- **2026-11-12 — A relaxed scan and an IRC open as their own cards: every scan point's optimized geometry, energy and forces, and every step of both reaction paths — instead of a convergence curve that plotted them as one optimisation** — A Gaussian relaxed scan is a sequence of *optimized structures*, one per scanned value, and an IRC is a sequence of structures along a reaction coordinate; neither is a series of optimizer steps. `src/parsers/logParser.ts` now reads both in the same single pass and returns them as `pathways`. A scan point is known by the `Step number … on scan point P out of T` line that precedes its tables: each point takes its own Berny table (its last step's energy, max/RMS force and max/RMS displacement), the geometry printed just above it becomes that point's frame (`scanPoint`/`scanStep`), and the file's `Summary of Optimized Potential Surface Scan` supplies the driven coordinate (`R(1,6)` in Å, `D(4,3,5,6)` in degrees) and a point whose run was cut short before printing its own table. A run that stopped early still lists the points it reached, with the total it set out to take. An IRC point is likewise named by `Point Number: N Path Number: P`. The reverse path's starting geometry has no point header of its own, so the `Beginning calculation of the REVERSE path.` banner binds it as point 0 of the next path; the `Point Number: 25` line the step limit announces but never prints a geometry for is dropped at `Maximum number of steps reached` instead of claiming the reverse start; and the geometry repeated after the summary is dropped as a copy of the last point. The `Nazarov` IRC now reads as exactly 25 + 25 points, every one with its own frame and energy, instead of a forward path with a phantom point 25 and a reverse path missing point 0. Each IRC calculation is also grouped as one **run**: the path halves it printed, the transition-state geometry printed above `Start new reaction path calculation` (marked so the input-orientation filter keeps it), and the direction each half was computed in — from the route's `irc=(…)`, the `Beginning calculation of the FORWARD/REVERSE path.` banner, or the `Calculation of … path complete.` line a file prints when its path hits the step limit. A `--Link1--` file that runs the analysis twice renumbers the second run's paths and keeps its own run and transition state, so two jobs can never merge into one chart.

  **The cards** — The convergence card's shell is reused because a file holds one of the three subjects. The chip reads **Scan** (with a drawn mark of sampled points along a potential) or **IRC / Path** (a valley–barrier–valley path), and the panel lists the points under their charts. A scan shows each point's driven coordinate, energy, max force and step count, plus a force chart at the optimized geometries. An IRC shows a calculation's **reverse and forward halves joined into one reaction path**: reverse points descending towards the barrier, the transition state, then forward points leaving it — one chart and one list rather than two charts whose barrier sits at opposite ends. Rows are named `R24 … R0`, `TS`, `F0 … F24` (the TS is tinted and clickable like any other row), and a file that computed one direction only is drawn with exactly that side, never with a mirrored copy; a `--Link1--` file gets one joined chart per IRC run. Rows take the 3D view to that point's structure, the chart's ring follows the frame on screen, x ticks use those same row names and the hover tooltip names `Reverse · point M`, `Transition state` or `Forward · point M`. A scan or IRC file never shows the ordinary convergence card, and an ordinary optimisation is untouched (no `pathways`, its convergence series and frame list stay as before).

  Verified with `.workbuddy/tools/verify_scan_irc.js` (**678 assertions**: the three `test/Nazarov/` scans at 32/19/1 points with their coordinates, energies, forces and frames; the cut-short scan's 1 of 21 points; the IRC's 25 + 25 points, point 0 of each path, the reverse start's energy, the dropped phantom point and trailing copy; the run grouping with its transition-state frame and energy; a `--Link1--`-style file with two IRC jobs keeping four paths in two runs with their own TS; forward-only and reverse-only synthetics keeping one run of 25 points; the client joining reverse → TS → forward (51 rows, label/frame/tooltip order, inferred directions when the file states none, and the one-sided forward and reverse cases); the untouched optimisation; and the client's row-name ticks and tooltips), plus `verify_log_optstep_link` 31, `verify_log_optsteps` 42, `verify_parser_hardening` 89, and headless Edge renders of the Scan, joined IRC, one-sided IRC and convergence pages.
- **2026-11-11 — Every large load reports a real percentage, not only a formatted checkpoint** — Opening a 59 MB xTB optimisation log froze the extension host for seconds with an indeterminate spinner (or nothing at all): the notification had no `increment`, the log parser reported no progress, and two full-text pre-scans ran before it. `src/webview/molecularViewer.ts` now gives the ordinary open a **determinate bar in whole percentage points**: reading owns 0–30% (reported per 1 MiB chunk as `Reading 59.0 MB · 45%`), decoding/preparation the next 5%, and the parser the rest. The phase also carries the number in its text (`Reading the output · 45%`), so the percentage is visible even where the bar itself is small. `src/parsers/logParser.ts`, `orcaOutParser.ts` and `xtbParser.ts` take an optional line callback (one step per 2048 lines), and structured-output detection reports one step per detector, so the bar keeps moving through the parse instead of sitting at a phase label. Files below **4 MiB** — **256 KiB** for `.log`/`.out`, whose single pass builds a frame per step — keep the old silent path, so an ordinary structure does not flash a notification; a VMD script is measured by the structures it names and reports `Loading molecule 2 of 5: <file>`. The second file **Diff** and **Import** read gets the same determinate read bar and parse bar, and a very large trajectory's page payload now reports its own integer steps — `Building frame 17 of 500`, then `Building the page payload` and `Assembling the page` — with a yield between frame batches, so the 650 000-atom page of a long optimisation log fills a bar instead of showing a spinner.

  **The pre-scan was the freeze** — A `.log`/`.out` is program output: `buildParseOptions` cannot read a companion file from it, but it used to test every LAMMPS/CP2K/ABACUS/ABINIT/ORCA family against the whole text, which cost seconds on a 60 MB file before the parser started. It now skips that scan for program outputs and plain coordinate files (a `.xyz` that is really a LAMMPS dump is kept by its name or its `ITEM: TIMESTEP` header), leaving the LAMMPS inputs/data/dumps, the DeepMD arrays and the program inputs on the old path. `isStructuredProgramOutput` likewise recognises an xTB trajectory, an ORCA output or a Gaussian log from cheap markers (`isFrameLogOutput`) before its sixteen full-text detectors, and reports per-detector progress only when those detectors actually run. Measured on the 59 MB `xtbopt1.log`: open 9.0 s → ≈0.5 s, and Diff reads in ≈50 ms followed by an ≈0.4 s parse with no silent gap.

  **Cancel means cancel** — the notification's button and the editor's own cancellation token both stop the load at its next check. A synchronous parse cannot be interrupted mid-pass, so its result is discarded at that check instead of being written into a document whose tab is gone; cancelling a Diff or Import posts the dismissal the page already understands rather than an error dialog. The multi-frame picker for a text trajectory is deliberately outside the notification (the parse and the picker are now two steps), so no spinner keeps running while the user chooses.

  **Nothing else changed** — a small structure still opens through the same `readFile` call and the same parser, a `.fchk` keeps its own byte-level reader and notification, and the parsed data is byte-identical. Verified with `.workbuddy/tools/verify_load_progress.js` (**37 checks** on the compiled host): the 59 MB `xtbopt1.log` opens with its 647 frames and a determinate bar made of whole points (68 increasing steps summing to 100, with `Reading the output · N%` labels), a 19 MB Gaussian optimisation log opens with its 139 frames and a determinate 68-step bar, a 1.2 MB optimisation log is above the 256 KiB log threshold and also reports a determinate bar, a small XYZ opens with no notification at all, a light VMD scene stays silent while a scene naming a large log reports `Loading molecule 1 of 1`, Cancel from the notification aborts an open with `CancellationError`, an already-cancelled editor token aborts before any notification, an `.fchk` still opens with its own `reading` notification, a LAMMPS input still opens the data file it names, a LAMMPS dump and a bare `POSCAR` still open, a 250 000-atom synthetic page reports a determinate 38-step `preparing the 3D view` bar with `Building frame` labels, Diff and Import each show a determinate read bar and a determinate 100-step parse bar and post their result, cancelling either posts `cancelled` instead of an error, a small Diff/Import keeps its silent path, and Diff refuses a checkpoint without reading it (the later Fixed entry below makes Diff read it with the streaming reader). `probe_fchk.js` (108 MB and 847 MB checkpoints), `verify_orca.js` (173 assertions) and the client-script syntax check stay green.

- **2026-11-11 — The charge card becomes the electronic-structure card: charges, the solvent cavity and the molecular orbitals as three tabs, and a formatted checkpoint opens with its orbitals drawn as isosurfaces** — A Gaussian job's electronic structure is one subject read three ways, so the **Charges** card (added earlier today) is now the **Electronic Structure** card behind the **Properties** chip, with a tab row of *Charges / Cavity / Orbitals*. The two disabled placeholders the old card carried ("reserved for the next version") are gone: both are real. Only the tabs a file can actually fill are enabled, so a log with a population analysis and no cavity shows two, and the card opens on the first one that has data.

  **Orbitals in a log** — `src/parsers/logParser.ts` reads the eigenvalue dumps in the same single pass: `Alpha  occ. eigenvalues --` and the `virt.` block that follows, then the beta pair, one dump per SCF. A dump is retired when its alpha *virtual* block starts — the only boundary the file states — so the last complete one is the converged SCF's, and a log cut off inside its occupied block still shows what it printed. The *Orbitals* tab draws those levels on a canvas (HOMO red, LUMO green, occupied levels filled, virtual ones outlined, tick labels in eV against the HOMO, the HOMO–LUMO gap below) and lists HOMO−6 … LUMO+6 with the orbital number, its energy in eV and its occupation; two arrows shift that window, because a 4680-orbital job cannot be scrolled, and a **MO #** box jumps straight to any orbital's own number (1 = the first the file prints), highlighting it and refusing a number past the last with the valid range in the placeholder. Occupations are the file's own occupied/virtual split — two electrons per orbital when no beta block was printed, one per spin orbital otherwise — and nothing finer is claimed.

  **A formatted checkpoint opens** — `src/parsers/fchkParser.ts` reads `.fchk`/`.fch`. It streams the bytes and never builds a string: a 432-atom checkpoint is 850 MB, and its MO coefficient block alone is 333 MB of text (4680² numbers). The small arrays are collected (atomic numbers and coordinates, every per-atom population whose name ends in *Charges* and whose length is the atom count, orbital energies, the contraction shells, the PCM sphere radii and centres, the energies and the RMS force); the coefficient block is skipped by counting its lines, and what is kept is where it starts, how many values a line carries and how wide a field is. That is what lets one orbital be read back later as a single contiguous 70 kB run of the file — the block is written in Fortran order, so an orbital's coefficients are adjacent — instead of holding 175 MB of doubles. The layout is verified while it streams past (every line the same width, the field width dividing it), and a file whose lines are not uniform is refused rather than addressed at a guessed offset. Occupations are rebuilt from the electron counts and the spin kind: two per orbital for a closed shell, one per spin orbital for UHF, the standard ROHF pattern when `IROHF` is set. `.fchk` joins the custom-editor selector, both context menus and the open dialog; a comparison refuses one with a message instead of reading a gigabyte as text.

  **The isosurfaces** — `src/tools/orbitalMesh.ts` turns a coefficient column into a picture. Each shell is expanded into its Cartesian components (Gaussian's own order: `px,py,pz`; `xx,yy,zz,xy,xz,yz`; `xxx,yyy,zzz,xyy,xxy,xxz,xzz,yzz,yyz,xyz`), the five pure d and seven pure f functions are written as the documented mixtures of the six and ten Cartesian ones, and each contraction is normalized by its own analytic self-overlap — the step that makes the result independent of the writer's convention, since a basis whose coefficients already describe normalized contractions gets a factor of 1. Within a shell the components are not normalized alike: a normalized `xy` primitive is √3 times a normalized `xx` one, and the six Cartesian components are not mutually orthogonal (`xx·yy` is a third of `xx·xx`), so each component carries its own factor and every pure d/f row is renormalized in that metric. The field is then summed **shell by shell over the grid points inside that shell's decay radius**, not point by point over every shell: for a 432-atom basis that is a hundredfold saving. The surface is marching *tetrahedra* (the cube split into six, sixteen cases, no 256-entry table to own and audit), each vertex carrying the field gradient as its normal so the mesh shades smoothly without welding. The default isovalue is 0.02 a.u. and the grid is Coarse / Normal / Fine at 0.62 / 0.42 / 0.30 Bohr (0.33 / 0.22 / 0.16 Å), a third finer than the first cut and with 2.5 / 5 / 9 million point ceilings, so the surface reads as a smooth field rather than a set of facets; the grid coarsens automatically until its point count fits the ceiling. Up to **ten** orbitals can be selected; each gets its own lobe colour pair, because two orbitals drawn at once must not share a phase colour where they overlap. Verified against the files themselves: the d-richest occupied orbital of the 147-atom checkpoint integrates to 1.00002 at 0.25 Bohr and its frontier orbitals to 1.0001, and every component of a synthetic s/p/6D/5D/10F/7F shell integrates to 1 — without the per-component factors that orbital read 0.982, which is the check that the normalization is right (a core 1s reads low only because a coarse grid steps over its peak).

  **It is not instant, and the card says so** — Sampling a 4680-function orbital on a million points is about a second of arithmetic, so the work runs in the extension host (where the coefficients are) in slices of ~28 ms with a yield between them, the same shape as the Molecular Mechanics pump: the panel keeps a progress bar with the phase (*Sampling the field*, *Cutting the surface*), the orbital being computed and the percentage, and Cancel or a new request is seen while the grid is filling. A new request supersedes the one in flight and a closed tab cancels it. Each orbital's mesh is posted — base64 of its typed arrays — as soon as it is cut, so a ten-orbital run fills in one lobe pair at a time and no single message carries the whole batch; an orbital whose field never reaches the isovalue says "no surface at this isovalue" in the legend while the rest are drawn. Measured with the finer grids: 140 ms to open the 108 MB / 147-atom checkpoint and 0.94 s for the 847 MB / 432-atom one; both lobes of a HOMO take 0.11 / 0.25 / 0.61 s (147 atoms) and 0.36 / 2.3 / 3.6 s (432 atoms) on Coarse / Normal / Fine.

  **The cavity is the file's cavity** — The *Cavity* tab names the **solvation model** first (`SMD`, `PCM`, `CPCM`, `IEFPCM`, … from the route's `SCRF=`; the continuum banner itself says PCM for every member of that family), then the cavity type, the solvent, the sphere count and the area and volume it measured; a checkpoint whose route states no model is labelled PCM, which is the family its `PCM-*` sphere arrays belong to. **Draw cavity** cuts the outer envelope of the unions out of a grid (the surface GePol builds, before its smoothing) as one translucent mesh. A checkpoint lists its spheres, so that is what is drawn; a log states the model, the solvent, the sphere count and the area and volume it measured, and the tab says the radii live in the checkpoint rather than inventing a set.

  **A stronger charge ramp** — The charge colour map keeps its symmetric red–grey–blue meaning but is lifted by a 0.78 power and runs to saturated ends (`#FF331A` red, `#1A33FF` blue), so a charge a small fraction of the table's largest magnitude is visibly red or blue instead of nearly grey; the legend draws the same ramp, and +q and −q of equal magnitude swap red and blue exactly.

  **A charge table takes the view to its geometry** — In an optimisation log every charge table belongs to one SCF's frame. Choosing a scheme, or switching on the values or the colour map, now switches the view to that frame — the same move a played normal mode makes — instead of leaving the panel to explain that nothing can be painted; the undo history is carried across when both frames hold the same atom list, so the jump cannot throw away an edit. A table whose atom list is not the one on screen still refuses (no frame can fix that), and navigating away afterwards paints nothing and brings back the **Show frame n** button.

  **A surface describes one geometry** — The meshes are computed from the file's own coordinates, so the page fingerprints the geometry when they arrive and compares it on every rebuild: an edit, a frame switch or an import retires them instead of leaving a lobe floating over atoms that moved. The charge map already refuses to paint across a mismatch; this is the same rule for a picture that cannot be recoloured.

  Verified with `.workbuddy/tools/verify_orbital_page.js` (61 checks in a real browser: the tab row and which tabs a log and a checkpoint enable, HOMO/LUMO marking, the window arrows and the MO # jump, the ten-orbital selection cap and the one-orbital-at-a-time messages, the request the page posts, a real base64 mesh pushed back and built into the scene, the group re-placed at the rotation centre across a `rebuildScene`, Clear, the cavity tab on both files with its solvation model, the log's "coefficients live in the .fchk" note) plus `probe_fchk.js`, `probe_mo_mesh.js` (section walk, column decode, ∫ψ², both test checkpoints) and `verify_fchk_edges.js` 21 (the last coefficient column, a CRLF block read column by column, a file with no final newline, a truncated file, a file that is not a checkpoint, and every component of a synthetic s/p/6D/5D/10F/7F shell integrating to 1), and the existing suites — `verify_charge_page` 105 (including the auto-jump on display and the Show-frame fallback), `verify_log_charges` 180, `verify_panel_layout` 925. A/B on a 40 MB log: the two new literal tests per banner line cost 5% of the parse, inside the budget the charge reader already set.

- **2026-11-11 — A Gaussian single-point job shows its atomic charges: a panel that chooses the scheme the file printed, colours the atoms by it, and writes the number on each atom** — A job that asks for a population analysis prints one table per scheme (Mulliken, ESP, APT, Hirshfeld, …), and `src/parsers/logParser.ts` now reads them out of the same single pass: a table's own name is its label, its rows give one charge per atom number, and a table that sums hydrogens into heavy atoms keeps the atoms it omits as `null` instead of a guessed zero. A block is recognised by its shape — a line naming charges, then rows of *(atom number, label, one number)* — so a program or a version that adds a scheme is read without a table to update, while a settings line that merely mentions charges ("Polarization charges : Total charges.") and a multi-column table (Hirshfeld's spin densities) are refused rather than misread. Only the last table of each scheme is kept: an optimisation that prints charges at every step keeps the last, and a `--Link1--` file keeps the last job's, because the panel describes the structure on screen.

  The new **Charges** card (opened from its chip, in the same top-left stack as Convergence / Modes / Route) lists exactly the schemes the file states, with the atom count a partial table covers; **Show the value on every atom** writes the charge over each atom, and **Colour the atoms by charge** paints them on a ramp that is symmetric about zero — red negative, grey at zero, blue positive, the depth set by |q| against the largest magnitude in the table, so a zero-charge atom is the same grey in every file. Half bonds take their atom's colour, the legend states the scale, and the stat line gives the range and the sum (which reproduces the charge the file states). Both switches are view state, like the scalar (Beta) map: nothing is written into an atom record, the colour is computed at build time, and turning one map on turns the other off. A value is drawn only onto the atom it was read for — the host sends the element of every atom a table covers, so an edited structure or a frame of another job's molecule shows nothing instead of somebody else's number, and the panel says why. A value is also quoted in the hover tooltip (`· q -0.6548 e (Mulliken charges)`). A table belongs to the geometry it was computed for, so it is drawn on that geometry only: on another frame nothing is painted and the card names both frames and offers **Show frame n**, which is the same frame switch the toolbar arrows use. The two switches keep their state across the jump, so the values appear the moment the right frame is on screen — painting the converged structure's charges onto step 1 would read as step 1's charges, which is the one thing a charge map must never do. The card carries two disabled rows — **Solvent cavity surface** and **Molecular orbitals** — reserved for the next version, so the controls will not move when they arrive.

- **A log's headline results open as a Job Summary card** — **Summary**, beside **Charges**, lists what a reader checks before looking at the structure: every SCF energy under the file's own label (`E(RPM7)`, `E(RwB97XD)`, …) with its Hartree value, **RMS Gradient Norm** (the gradient line only — `RMS Force = .10134D-02` is the SCF's DIIS test and is not a gradient), the dipole moment with its total and X/Y/Z components in Debye, the point group (`Point Group = C1` and `Full point group  C2V  NOp 4`), charge/multiplicity, atoms and basis functions, SCF cycles, **Job cpu time** and **Elapsed time** in the file's own words, and the termination line (an error termination is marked in red). A `--Link1--` file shows one block per job, numbered. **Which structure the block describes is stated, not implied**: every geometry-level value keeps the last one the job printed, so the block is the job's *last SCF* — for a finished optimisation that is the converged geometry, not the frame on screen — and the note under it names that SCF. A value the file only printed for an earlier step (an optimisation that was cut short prints no analysis for its last geometry, so its dipole is the first step's) is not silently shown beside a later energy: the row carries an amber `SCF n` mark, and a second line says the file only printed it there. The job-level rows — atoms and basis functions, SCF cycles, both runtimes, the termination — describe the job as a whole and are never marked. Every field is what the file states: nothing is derived, defaulted or converted, a field the file omits is not shown at all, and a value keeps the digits it was printed with — the number is formatted from its shortest round-trip form, not through `toFixed`, whose last digit differs between engine versions for a value of a few thousand. Numbers are not re-rounded, so `-5260.65933886` reads exactly as the log wrote it.

- **The single pass stays one pass** — the charge tables and the summary lines are read by the scan that already collects frames, convergence steps and frequencies, behind a two-character gate (an indented banner line starting with a capital) so coordinate rows, frequency tables and eigenvalue dumps pay one comparison each; the four literal tests that used to compile a regex per line are hoisted to module constants, and the external-energy pattern is now preceded by the literal it already required. A log without charges or a summary reads exactly as before and shows neither card, and the charge tables leave every other field of a file untouched.

### Changed

- **2026-11-11 — The webview is split into host modules and a shipped page, with the rendered page unchanged** — `src/webview/molecularViewer.ts` had grown to **25 233 lines / 1.27 MB**, of which roughly 20 000 lines were the page's client script inside a template literal: a file no editor can navigate and no parser can check. It is now **2 007 lines**: the editor provider, its document type and the MM run state remain there, and everything else moved to focused modules — `loading.ts` (progress notification, chunked read, text decoding), `payloads.ts` (the payload shapes and the element colours), `parseSetup.ts` (LAMMPS/CP2K/ABINIT/ABACUS/ORCA include expansion, companion resolution, `buildParseOptions`), `trajectoryDocuments.ts` (binary and text trajectories, topology pairing, frame pickers), `structureLoader.ts` (`loadStructureFile`), `vmdPayload.ts` (egg metadata and the resolved VMD scene payload), `importHelpers.ts` (the Import dialog and copy block) and `page.ts` (`renderViewerPage`, which builds the JSON payload and assembles the HTML).

  The page itself is shipped rather than embedded: `media/viewer.css` (**54 kB**) and `media/viewer.js` (**20 095 lines / 964 kB**) are read beside `media/three.min.js` and inlined with the same nonce, and the document payload reaches the client through a nonce'd `window.__MV_HOST__` block instead of `${jsonData}` interpolation in the template. A damaged installation still opens the static "could not read the viewer assets" page, naming the file it could not read. No behaviour changed: for the same document the new page's stylesheet, body markup and client body are identical to the pre-split page (54 198 / 16 749 / 963 435 characters compared), and the page renders a 147-atom Gaussian job in headless Edge with the real WebGL renderer — a 1170×608 drawing buffer, no error panel, no uncaught script error, and the info panel naming 147 atoms (`verify_refactor_page.js`, **17 checks**, including a crystal page that still offers Polyhedra and carries its base atoms). `verify_load_progress.js` stays green (**37 checks**, including Diff/Import and the 250 000-atom page), `npm run compile` is clean, and `.vscodeignore` still packages `media/viewer.css`, `media/viewer.js` and `media/three.min.js`.

### Fixed

- **2026-11-18 — Twelve defects in the crystallographic tools and the module-source guards: an O–H oxygen that summed 1.2, thermal ellipsoids read in the wrong basis, an R cell at α = 90° taken for hexagonal, half-occupied sites over-bonded, and a source guard that could not see a missing constant (version 1.0.5; the version number is not bumped)** — `src/tools/bvs.ts`, `src/tools/adp.ts`, `src/parsers/cifParser.ts`, `src/tools/spaceGroupTable.ts`, `src/tools/bondDetector.ts`, `src/tools/crystalGeometry.ts`, `src/tools/periodicTable.ts`, `src/tools/bondPerception.ts`, `src/webview/page.ts` and `media/viewer.js`.

  **A hydrogen is a bond-valence cation now** — H had no row in the cation table, so every O–H, N–H and F–H contact was skipped: a hydroxide or water oxygen summed only its cation contacts (typically 1.0–1.3) and was reported as badly under-coordinated, although its O–H bond is exactly the missing valence. H is added as H+ with the fits the accumulated parameter file lists — H–O 0.907/0.28 (Brown's O–H < 1.05 Å branch, the one that covers the covalent bond; the file's other two branches describe hydrogen bonds), H–N 1.014/0.413, H–F 0.708/0.558, H–S 1.192/0.591 and H–Cl 1.336/0.53 — so an O–H contact at 0.96 Å now contributes 0.83 to both ends. Hydride H(−1) is deliberately **not** inferred: an oxidation state guessed from an element symbol is the kind of guess this table exists to avoid, so a hydride site is left alone rather than checked as H+.

  **Occupancy weights a contact** — a contact is scaled by the occupancy of the neighbour that makes it, because a site present in only half the cells cannot contribute a full bond to the sites around it (a half-occupied neighbour therefore contributes half a bond and still one contact); a neighbour at occupancy zero is not a contact at all. The central site's own occupancy is not applied to its sum: the check is conditional on that site being there, and when it is, the neighbours' occupancies are what its coordination sphere contains. An occupancy outside [0, 1] is a file error and is read as full rather than allowed to amplify a bond.

  **The cutoff reaches the Cs–I tail** — `BVS_MAX_CUT` was 4.0 Å, but Cs–I's own per-pair cutoff is 3.18 + 3.3 × 0.37 = 4.401 Å, so the longest tabulated pair was clipped and Cs/Ba–I sums lost their far contacts. The ceiling is now 4.5 Å, which bounds the lattice search without truncating any tabulated pair.

  **A CIF's Uij are crystal-axis components, not Cartesian** — `src/tools/adp.ts` gains `uCifAsCart`/`uCartAsCif`: scale by the reciprocal cell lengths (`u*_ii = r_i² u_ii`, `u*_ij = r_i r_j u_ij`), rotate by the lattice matrix (`U_cart = Mᵀ u* M`), and divide back out for saving. `cifParser.ts` converts every `_atom_site_aniso_U_ij` record as it is read; a cell with no usable reciprocal basis leaves the record out with a count and a warning instead of drawing an ellipsoid derived from a division by zero. The CIF writer (`cifUFromCart` in `media/viewer.js`) applies the inverse, so a non-orthogonal cell saves the tensor it read rather than a rotated one; an orthogonal cell's numbers pass through unchanged, which is why the old writer looked correct until a monoclinic or hexagonal cell was saved and read back.

  **An R cell at α = 90° is still rhombohedral** — `looksRhombohedral` rejected an α near 90°, but the primitive cell of an R lattice passes through exactly 90° at c/a = √(3/2), so that cell was handed to the hexagonal basis — the one choice guaranteed to be wrong for a cell that is not hexagonal. The signature is now three equal lengths and three equal angles with no restriction on their value; a hexagonal cell cannot satisfy it, because its γ is 120°.

  **A name that leaves the setting open offers the other one** — `lookupSpaceGroupOps` takes an optional `setting` (`'1'`/`'2'` for the two origins, `'h'`/`'r'` for an R group's axes) and every entry carries `alternatives`: the same group under the remaining settings, each a complete operations list. A suffix the name itself states (`P n n n:1`, `R -3 c:H`) still wins over the caller's request, because that is the file's own statement; `assumed`/`assumedOrigin` are now true only when neither the name nor the request settled the point. `lookupSpaceGroupSettings` accepts the same request and its entries carry no nested alternatives. The name-only default remains origin choice 2, the International Tables convention, but it is no longer the only setting reachable.

  **Every e-glide setting answers to its 2002 name** — the five aliases were hard-coded to the standard settings, so a modern spelling of another setting (`A e m a` for `A b m a`, setting cab of No. 64) was unknown. The alias is now derived from each row: the affected symbol position follows the centring letter (A → position 1, B → position 2, C → position 3), only a single glide `a`/`b`/`c`/`d` in that position becomes `e`, and the five original spellings still resolve exactly as before.

  **The BVS guard sees bare constants, not only exports** — it looked for `exports.` qualifiers, while the compiled bodies also reference module constants bare (`BVS_TAIL`, not `exports.BVS_TAIL`), so a constant used but never emitted became a free variable in the page. Every `BVS_*`/`bvs*` identifier in each emitted body is now checked against a set derived from the same tables and function list the emitter uses; a name the page would not define is refused at module load, not when a panel opens.

  **A script body that would end its own element is refused, and bond perception gets the binding check BVS had** — both builders now refuse a `</script` sequence (the HTML parser ends a script element at the first occurrence, even inside a string or comment), and `page.ts` checks both module sources and both shipped media scripts before interpolating them. The bond-perception builder had no binding check at all: it now strips comments, collects each body's own locals and parameters, and refuses any uppercase constant or `bp*` call that is neither a `MODULE_CONSTANTS` entry nor a `MODULE_FUNCTIONS` name, so a function that references something outside the shipped scope fails the build instead of failing in a browser.

  **An explicit empty bond table is a bond table** — `ensureBonds` tested `hasExplicitBonds && bonds.length > 0`, so a file that states it has no bonds (a MOL2 `@<TRIPOS>BOND` block with no records, an empty connect section) fell through to distance perception and invented bonds the file had explicitly denied. `hasExplicitBonds` is now honoured on its own, and the authored (empty) list stays the list.

  **A coefficient in front of a variable is a coefficient** — `parseSymOp` accepted only ±1 before `x`/`y`/`z`, so `2x` silently became `x + 2`: the coefficient was dropped and the 2 added to the origin, producing an operation that moves every atom. An integer number (optionally followed by `*`) directly before a variable is now read as its coefficient, so `2x`, `-2*x` and `x+2y` parse as written; a fractional number followed by a variable keeps its translation reading rather than inventing a non-lattice rotation.

  **A residue name is not an element** — `elementFromAtomName` matched the longest known prefix and then the first letter, so every three-letter residue name became an element: `SOL` sulfur, `WAT` tungsten, and a bare `CA` calcium even in a protein. The rules now follow the label's shape: one letter is that element; a two-letter label with a lowercase second letter is a symbol (`Si`); a serial after it marks element sites (`ZN1`, `CA1`); otherwise a two-letter label is a PDB atom name whose element is its first letter, and a suffix outside the atom-name set (`CL`, `NA`, `FE`) keeps its two-letter symbol. A label of three or more letters is an element only when its first two letters are a symbol (`SIL` → Si); `SOL`, `WAT`, `TIP` (the TIPn water models would otherwise be titanium) and `HOH` (holmium) now answer nothing, while the peptide terminal oxygen `OXT` is still oxygen. `OW` → O and `HW1` → H are unchanged.

  Verified with `npm run compile` clean and the compiled modules: `.workbuddy/tools/verify_bvs.js` (**188 assertions** — the H row's states, pairs and cutoff, the O–H contribution at both ends, occupancy 0.5 halving a bond while keeping one contact, occupancy 0 refusing a contact, Cs–I at 4.35 Å counted under the 4.5 Å ceiling, the source running in a bare context with no free names, and the page checking the BVS source before embedding it), `verify_adp.js` (**93** — CIF→Cartesian against an independent `Mᵀ D U D M` derivation, the symmetry rotation, a 3-fold cycle returning the converted tensor, and an isotropic hexagonal tensor staying diagonal), `verify_space_group.js` (**189** — α = 90° and α = 89.95° R cells choosing the rhombohedral basis, name-only origin choice 2 with origin 1 in `alternatives`, explicit origin and axis requests honoured, a name's own suffix winning over the request, and the e-glide names of non-standard settings such as `A e m a`), and `verify_reader_fixes.js` (**143** — `C c c e` yielding all four of its settings; `SOL`/`WAT`/`TIP`/`TIP3`/`HOH` naming no element while `CA`/`CA1`/`CL`/`NA`/`FE`/`SIL`/`OXT`/`OW`/`HW1` keep theirs; `2x`, `-2*x+1/2` and `x+2y` parsing as coefficients with their translations intact; and an explicit empty bond list staying empty while a list that states nothing still perceives its bond).

- **2026-11-18 — Bond perception and orbital isosurfaces: hydrogen molecules that never bonded, orbitals drawn at the origin, ring alkenes exported as aromatic, and the valence solver's worst cases — fifteen defects, none of which changes a structure whose geometry was already read correctly (version 1.0.5; the version number is not bumped)** — `src/tools/bondPerception.ts`, `src/tools/orbitalMesh.ts` and `src/parsers/fchkParser.ts`.

  **Hydrogen bonds at all** — `PAIR_CUT.HH` was `0.0`, so no H–H pair could pass the cutoff: H₂, a bridging hydride and a dihydrogen ligand came out as separate atoms with no bond, no valence and no force-field term. The entry is now 1.1 Å — longer than every bonded H···H (0.74 Å in H₂, 0.9 Å in a dihydrogen complex) and shorter than the shortest hydrogen bond — so H₂ bonds and a hydrogen-bond contact still does not.

  **Orbitals are placed by the map when the file states no shell coordinates** — `buildEvalShells` read a shell's centre as `basis.centers[s * 3] || 0`, so a checkpoint whose shell-coordinate array was missing, or a record that was not finite, drew the whole orbital as one blob at the origin. A shell now takes its centre from the file's own coordinates when they are finite and complete, falls back to the Shell-to-atom map plus the structure's coordinates converted from Angstrom to Bohr, and **fails naming the shell** when neither source can place it. The map itself was kept exactly as Gaussian writes it (1-based) while every atom index in the viewer is 0-based, so the fallback landed on the next atom; `fchkParser.ts` converts it once on read.

  **The coefficient column is checked before it is read** — the shell walk advanced the MO offset by the count its own code assumed and never compared the total with the file's basis-function count, so a truncated column or a mismatched pure/Cartesian reading shifted every shell after the mistake and drew a wrong orbital with no error. Both readings are now summed: the per-shell sign first (the file's own statement, and what Gaussian itself writes), and the job-level `Pure/Cartesian d/f` scalars only when the signs do not account for the stated count (the writer that codes every d shell as Cartesian while its header calls them pure). A g shell or an unknown code fails per shell with its type named, and the offset advances by the number of coefficients the shell **actually consumed** — five for a pure d rescued by the scalar, not six — which is the one remaining shift the first version of this check would otherwise have introduced.

  **A 1.5 bond must lie on a ring of aromatic bonds, not merely on a ring** — the C–C band that brackets 1.34–1.44 Å describes both an aromatic bond and a double bond, so a cyclohexene's alkene, a ring imine, a furan's C=C and a conjugated diene all took 1.5 and were exported to MOL2 as `ar`. The aromatic subgraph (1.5 edges alone) is now tested for bridges in one iterative Tarjan pass: an edge that is not a bridge closes a loop of aromatic bonds and keeps 1.5; a bridge is downgraded to the best non-aromatic order its length supports (a band that brackets the distance wins over the nearest tabulated length, because 1.44 Å is inside the single band and nearer the double ideal). Benzene, pyridine, pyrrole, thiophene, naphthalene and porphyrin rings are untouched; a cyclohexene, a ring imine, furan's Kekulé form and cyclooctatetraene come back as the single/double pattern they are. Because a single bond can never join the aromatic subgraph, a trajectory contact appearing or disappearing near the cutoff no longer relabels bonds elsewhere in the structure — the flicker the whole-graph test produced.

  **The valence solver has a work budget, not only a combination count** — `MAX_COMBOS` bounded the number of assignments but not the cost of each (a matching round is O(k³), and the loop could run 200 rounds per assignment), so a component near the cap could occupy the host for an unbounded time. A second budget (`MAX_COMBO_WORK`, 40 million element operations) is charged before each assignment and each matching round, the matching itself is bounded by both 200 rounds and `4 + 2k`, and a component that exhausts it declines with `work-budget` — reported as `declinedByWork` — instead of hanging. The per-component index map is also allocated once per call rather than once per component, which was O(atoms × components) on a structure of many fragments.

  **No length evidence is not perfect evidence** — `bpSpecDev` answered 0 for an element pair with no table entry, so a Si–O, S–O or metal–O bond read as fitting every order perfectly and the refinement was free to move it by half a unit; that is where the half-integer bonds came from. It now answers −1, and every caller treats it as "this bond cannot be moved on length grounds". The tables themselves gained the pairs they were missing: S–O (with a single-bond band wide enough to bracket a sulphate's 1.47 Å, so the relaxation from four double bonds has somewhere to go), P–O, Si–O and Cl–O; the halogens' ceiling is no longer 1 but 7 (with 3/4/5/7 in the valence set), so perchlorate, chlorate and the chlorine fluorides are not permanently hypervalent. `Z_OF` now covers the transition and inner-transition metals; a metal gets no valence model, so it is **frozen** inside the solve (its target is its own degree, so no bond of its is moved) instead of disabling the whole component — a ferrocene's rings are solved again — and its contacts take a single-bond length from the covalent radii.

  **Formal charges: a nitrogen at four is not always a cation, and a three-coordinate carbon is counted, not encountered** — the formal-charge formula did not know how many neighbours a centre had, so a pyrrole N (valence 4 over three neighbours) was charged +1, and a dative sulphoxide or a sulphone S was charged +1/+2. `bpAtomicCharge` now takes the degree: a nitrogen at valence 4 is +1 only with four neighbours, a sulphur at 3 over 3 is the dative S(+1), at 3 over 2 is a neutral thiophene, at 4 over 3 a neutral sulphoxide and at 6 neutral whatever the split. The solver's own charge check sums the base total first and adds the three-coordinate carbons afterwards by count, so renumbering a component can no longer change whether its assignment balances; and the solver's returned charges take the component's own degree rather than that of whatever map happened to be around.

  **The rest is reported instead of silent** — `charges` is now one entry per atom whatever the options say (it was filled only when the diagnostics were asked for, contradicting its own interface, while it costs nothing); the `MAX_RELAX_ATOMS` skip is visible as `relaxSkipped` and a component held back by `maxSolverAtoms` as `oversizeComponents`; and a solver decline says whether the combination ceiling or the work budget stopped it.

  **The quad in a four-crossing tetrahedron is split along a true diagonal** — the two triangles were cut along the first pair of crossing points, which can be two neighbours of the quad rather than opposite corners, folding the two triangles over each other and leaving a pinhole in the surface. The split now finds the two pairs of crossing edges that share no tetrahedron corner (the quad's true diagonals), takes the shorter one, and cuts along it; a brute-force check over all six four-crossing masks confirms exactly two such pairs exist in every case. The mesh winding remains deliberately unfixed — every vertex carries its own normal and the material draws both faces.

  Verified with `npm run compile` clean and a scratch Node harness over the compiled modules (**33 bond cases and 19 orbital cases**): H₂ at 0.74 Å; benzene (six 1.5 bonds, zero charges); cyclohexene (one double bond, no half orders); pyrrole and pyridine (aromatic C–N, N charge 0); DMSO and sulphone (S=O double, S charge 0); sulphate (S valence 6 after relaxation, total −2); perchlorate (Cl–O single, total −1, Cl +3); silicate (Si–O single); ferrocene (ten Fe–C single, Fe charge null, no half orders); acetate and an imine at 1.36 Å (not 1.5); Fe–O single; the acetate solver invariant under atom renumbering; a 400-atom cluster under 5 s; and `.charges` filled without `extras`. For the orbitals: sign-coded s/6D/5D shells; a Cartesian-coded d rescued by `cartesianD=false` producing exactly the pure reading of the same five coefficients; the shell-to-atom fallback in Bohr; the two refusal cases (no centre source, mismatched column); a real `methane.fchk` with its 0-based map; a marching-tetrahedra sphere whose vertices sit on the sphere and whose enclosed volume is right; and every four-crossing tetrahedron's two diagonals.

- **2026-11-17 — Six places where the page was told less than the file said: the SCF a summary value came from, a Draw button for orbitals that cannot be drawn, a TCL scene's charges and summary, the charge of an import, the charge an undo left behind, and the undo history of the frame next door** — Each one is a number or a control that described something other than what was on screen; none of them changed how a file is read.

  **The Job Summary's SCF stamps** — `src/webview/page.ts` now forwards `LogSummary.scfOf` — the SCF (1-based, within its job) that last printed each geometry-level value — which the panel has been reading at `s.scfOf` since the card was added. A run that was cut short prints no analysis for its final step, so its dipole is the first step's while its energy is the last one's; without the stamps the two sat in one block as if they described one structure, and the amber `SCF n` mark never appeared. The three fields travel (`energy`, `rmsGradient`, `dipole`) and a summary that states none sends none, so nothing is invented. On `test/168791_1_opt1.log` — a 139-frame optimisation — the payload now reads `scfOf {energy: 139, dipole: 1}`, which is exactly the case the mark exists for: the energy is the converged step's and the dipole is the first step's.

  **Orbitals that cannot be drawn** — `canDraw` tested the coefficient layout alone. A basis with g functions, a checkpoint opened through the workspace API (no local path, so no column can be read back), and a UHF checkpoint whose second spin channel carries no coefficient block therefore kept the Draw button visible and the `reason` hidden — the request then failed in the host, after the click. `canDraw` now also requires `maxL <= 3` and `orbitals.path`, and the layout is answered **per spin**: `orbitals.spins[i].drawable` gates that channel's checkboxes, the iso/quality controls and the Draw button, and a channel without coefficients says so in place of the controls (`media/viewer.js`: `orbitalSpinDrawable`, `propsMoHtml`, `orbListHtml`, `requestOrbitalSurfaces`). A request whose selection belongs to an undrawable channel is refused in the page, with the reason, instead of travelling to a host that can only refuse it.

  **A TCL scene's charges, summary, thresholds and scan card** — `loadTclScene` has been returning `charges` and `summary` since the Summary card was added, and `loadStructureFile` has been returning `optThresholds` and `pathways` with every log; the `.tcl` branch copied only `optSteps`, `normalModes`, `routes` and `logSource`. Opening a Gaussian log *through a script* therefore showed no Summary card, no Charges card, no convergence threshold and no Scan/IRC card, while the same file opened directly showed all four. The branch now takes every field the loader returns (`optThresholds`, `charges`, `summary`, `pathways`), `LoadedStructure` declares `pathways`, and `loadTclScene` forwards it, so a scene and the file it names describe the same job.

  **The charge of an import** — `postImportCopies` multiplied the fragment's charge by the copies and sent the multiplicity as it stood, and the page adopted either value only when the canvas was empty: an import beside an existing structure dropped the incoming charge, and MM and Add H then worked from a total that described only half of what was on screen. The two blocks are placed apart and share no electrons, so the page now **adds** the charge (and adds it again on every import), while an import onto an empty canvas still adopts the file's own values. The multiplicity does not add — two open shells can couple either way — so the host states it only when the copies cannot differ (a closed shell stays a closed shell, a single copy is the fragment itself) and otherwise sends none with a note the status line shows: `N copies of a multiplicity-M fragment: the combined spin state is not stated by the file`. The panel's multiplicity follows the sum of the unpaired electrons when both sides state one, which is the high-spin limit and the only value no file has to be guessed for.

  **The charge an undo left behind** — The undo snapshot carried the atoms, the bonds, the crystal state and the provenance, but not `charge` / `multiplicity`: an import onto an empty canvas adopts the file's values, and undoing it cleared the atoms while the panel, the next bond perception and the next save kept describing the imported file's electronic state. `pushUndo` stores both values with the flags that say whether the file stated them at all (`hasCharge` / `hasMult` — `undefined` is "the file does not say", which is not zero and one), and `doUndo` puts them back with the geometry.

  **The undo history of the frame next door** — A frame switch cleared the stack, so an edit made three steps ago could not be undone after navigating away and back, even though the edit itself survives in the frame (`TRAJ_FRAMES[idx].atoms`, or a log frame's own records). The stack is now parked under the frame it was made on (`parkUndoHistory` / `adoptUndoHistory`, keyed by molecule and frame) and taken up again when that frame is visited, so a snapshot of one frame can never be applied to another and no history is thrown away by navigation. The parked histories are bounded by **atoms**, not by frames (`UNDO_PARK_MAX_ATOMS`, 120 000): six frames of a 5 000-atom trajectory would otherwise hold three hundred copies of it, so the oldest parked history goes first and the newest is always kept.

  Verified with `.workbuddy/tools/verify_editor_channels.js` (**138 checks**, including the payload-level assertions on `scfOf` and the four orbital cases — a log, a g basis, a checkpoint without a path, a UHF file with one coefficient block — and the session/undo-history logic run against the shipping source), `verify_log_charges` **231**, `verify_log_optsteps` **42**, `verify_log_optstep_link` **31**, `verify_parser_hardening` **91**, `verify_scan_irc` **724**, `verify_orca` **173**, and the green crystal suites (`verify_crystal` 254, `verify_crystal_edit` 155, `verify_crystal_addh` 58, `verify_crystal_bonds` 44).

- **2026-11-17 — Twelve SMILES → 3D and Add H defects: hydrogens that landed on each other, an enantiomer read backwards, an E/Z that flipped when the stereobond was written as a branch, and a ring that could not close** — `src/tools/smilesTo3d.ts` and `src/tools/addHydrogens.ts`. Every entry below is a place where the generator read less than the SMILES said, or placed atoms without looking at what was already around them; the geometries that were already right are unchanged, and the one number that changes everywhere is the iteration count, which falls wherever a structure used to be repaired after being built wrong.

  **A hydrogen sees its siblings** — the dihedral scan excluded every atom within two bonds from its placement energy, which is exactly the set a methyl's hydrogens belong to: every candidate angle scored zero, the scan stopped on the first one, and the three or four hydrogens of a centre were separated only by the hard-sphere term — methane came out with a 1.25 Å hydrogen pair, and a coincident pair was left for the relaxation, which skipped a zero-length constraint instead of pulling the two apart. A 1-3 pair now carries the distance its own bond angle implies (only where nothing else can separate the pair — a hydrogen's siblings, and equivalent terminal substituents such as CF₄'s four fluorines; applied to a ring-closing bond it pulled adamantane's cage out by 0.2 Å, which is why the criterion is narrow). The clash term follows it: the relaxation gives two coincident atoms a deterministic direction and pushes them half the target distance apart instead of skipping them, a placement that lands on an existing atom is rotated 60° at a time until it does not, and the geometry self-check gained an `overlaps` count that ignores topology — the old clash counter only looked at pairs more than four bonds apart, so the one case that needed watching was invisible to it.

  **The bracketed hydrogen is written after the centre, not before it** — the neighbour order of `@`/`@@` is the order the atoms appear in the SMILES, and `[C@H]` writes its hydrogen immediately after the atom symbol: after the preceding atom, before the branches. Putting it first made `[C@@H](C)` and `[C@@]([H])(C)` opposite enantiomers — the same molecule written two legal ways came out as two different compounds. The order is now recorded at parse time (whether the centre had a preceding atom at all), and the two spellings agree; `@` and `@@` still mirror each other.

  **A stereobond keeps its meaning when it is written as a branch** — `/` says "up as you move from the bond's first atom to its second", so the same symbol means opposite things depending on whether that single bond was written toward the double bond (`F/C=C/F`, trans) or away from it (`C(/F)=C/F`, cis). Comparing the raw symbols made the branch spelling the other isomer. The comparison is now made in a single frame — the direction pointing away from the alkene — after which equal means cis and different means trans, and fumaric and maleic acid come out at 180° and 0°.

  **Add H reads the charge the structure states** — the centre's target valence came from the neutral empirical table, so an `[O-]` with its single bond was given a hydrogen it does not have (and a bare fluoride would have been given one too). An atom that carries a formal charge now takes the valence that charge implies (group 15–17: one bond per positive charge, one fewer per negative; group 13 the other way; group 14 one fewer either way), a charge that leaves the centre with no bonds at all is reported rather than completed, and a structure that states no charge completes exactly as before. The panel passes the charge along when an atom has one.

  **A seven- or eight-membered ring closes at its own angle** — `ringAngleFor` knew 3, 4 and 5, so every larger ring was built with the hybridisation angle: a planar seven-ring cannot close at 120° and an eight-ring ended at 0.43 of the van der Waals contact with a 0.19 Å bond error (a torn ring). A ring that has to be planar — aromatic, or all sp² — now takes the polygon angle for its size, computed for anything past the table, while a saturated ring of the same size keeps the hybridisation angle and stays puckered. Tropylium, cyclooctatetraene and azulene are planar to 0.01 Å with bond errors at or below 0.05 Å; the dead `RING_ANGLE` table that held exactly these numbers and was never read is gone.

  **Chirality is not corrected by turning half a ring** — flipping a centre rotates two of its substituents by 180°, and on a ring centre a substituent reaches the *other* ring substituent through the ring, so the rotation dragged half the ring with it. Only branches that do not contain another substituent of the centre are used now; a bridgehead with nothing free but its hydrogen is left as built. A swap that does not reach the requested parity is undone, so a failed correction no longer leaves a structure that is neither the old configuration nor the new one.

  **The atom cap is read before the hydrogen graph is built** — `expandHydrogens` ran first, so a 20 000-atom string built 60 002 atoms and only then reported that 60 002 is over the limit. The count is the same (`hcount` is known before the expansion) and the message is unchanged, but the graph is no longer built to be thrown away.

  **A wide methylene is not an aromatic CH** — an Add H centre with two bonds and no stated orders was given one hydrogen whenever its angle passed 115°, which is every methylene of a large saturated ring (cyclooctane 117°) as well as an aromatic CH. The bond lengths decide it now: an aromatic C–C is 0.15 Å shorter than the single bond the table states, a saturated ring's bonds are that single bond, and bonds to hydrogen never count (an sp² C–H and an sp³ C–H are the same length). The angle stays as the second witness, and a stated bond order still wins over both.

  **`@TH1`, `@AL1` and the rest are names for the same centre** — the parser read `@` and `@@` and then rejected `[C@TH1H]` as a malformed bracket atom, although it is the same stereocentre written the long way. The classes are parsed (`TH`, `AL`, `SP`, `TB`, `OH`), `TH1`/`AL1` map to `@` and `TH2`/`AL2` to `@@`; the trigonal-bipyramidal, octahedral and square-planar classes describe a geometry this engine does not build, and are accepted with the parity their number implies rather than refusing the molecule.

  **An inter-ring bond between aromatic atoms is a single bond, and it is not pinned flat** — every unmarked bond between two aromatic atoms was promoted to aromaticity, including the biphenyl bond that joins two rings: `c1ccccc1c1ccccc1` came back with thirteen aromatic bonds, a 1.5 bond between two rings that MOL2 export and Molecular Mechanics then treated as aromatic. Aromaticity requires a ring, so a bond is promoted only when it is not a bridge (a single Tarjan pass, iterative), and the written and the implied spellings of biphenyl now agree. That exposed the second half of the same bond: once it is a real single bond between two sp² carbons, the conjugation rule pinned its dihedral at 180°, one rigid ring rotation per bond, and along a chain of rings the error compounds until the rings cannot close — a ten-ring chain reached a 3.4 Å "bond" with 94 overlaps (6.4 Å in the explicit spelling, where the flaw already existed). A bond that joins two rings is left to the non-bonded scan instead, which is what a biaryl bond does: a ten-ring chain stays within 0.09 Å on every bond with no two atoms on top of each other, and both spellings build the same geometry.

  **An isotope is in the formula and in the mass** — `isotope` was parsed, drawn in the atom table and then dropped: the formula counted `[13CH4]` as CH4 (16.04) and the molecular weight used the element's average mass. Isotopes are counted as their own nuclide, written as `[13C]H4` and `C[2H]4` (unbracketed they read as C₂H₄), weighed with an exact-mass table that falls back to the mass number for a nuclide it does not list, and kept in the Hill order of their element.

  **A ring closure whose two ends state different bonds is an error** — `C=1CCCCC-1` states a double bond at one end and a single bond at the other; the second one silently won, so the structure was neither what the string asked for nor an error. Both ends are now compared and a disagreement is reported (`conflicting_ring_bond` at the closing end) while `C=1CCCCC=1`, `C1CCCCC=1` and a bare closure keep working.

  Verified with a new harness, `.workbuddy/tools/verify_smiles_defects.js` (**273 checks** on the compiled modules, one section per defect above): the hydrogens of methane, methyl, methylene, ethane, ammonia, ammonium and CF₄ measured pairwise before any relaxation; the implicit and explicit spellings of four stereocentres compared by parity; six E/Z cases including fumaric and maleic acid, plus an unspecified double bond; Add H on `[O-]`, `[F-]`, `[N+]`, a neutral O and an over-valent anion; tropylium, cyclooctatetraene, azulene and a ten-membered ring checked for planarity, collapse and bond error, and a saturated seven-ring checked for keeping sp³ angles; five ring stereocentres and a bridgehead measured for ring bonds and 1-3 distances; a 20 000-atom string refused with the expanded count and a timing bound; a 117° methylene, an aromatic octagon at 1.39 Å, the same octagon at 1.54 Å and a stated double bond; five extended classes against `@`/`@@`; biphenyl in both spellings; biaryl chains of 6, 10 and 20 rings in both spellings; five isotope cases; the four ring-closure spellings; the overlap counter; and a battery of 21 molecules whose formulas, clashes and bond deviations are asserted. The harnesses beside it stay green: `verify_addh_orders` **32**, `verify_crystal_addh` **58**, `verify_mm_ff` **78**, `verify_mm_switch` **22**, `verify_mm_hybrid` **22**, `verify_arom_dash` **2834**, `verify_frame_fix` **41**, and the page script still parses (20 763 lines). Against the 1.0.5 build the same battery shows no regression: bicyclo[2.2.2]octane 0.115 → 0.004 Å, naphthalene written as a Kekulé string 0.174 → 0.015 Å, cyclooctatetraene 0.181 → 0.043 Å with its one clash gone, methane's relaxation 19 → 2 iterations, and every other molecule within 0.005 Å of what it was. The biaryl chains are the one place a number moves the other way, and for a reason worth naming: the implied inter-ring bond is now a real single bond and is no longer held flat, so on a forty-ring chain its bond error rises from 0.11 to 0.14 Å while its clashes fall from 67 to 35; the explicit spelling, which already had the flat-bond flaw, falls from 6.45 Å to 0.14 Å.

- **2026-11-17 — Seventeen force-field defects: a chemistry the field could not see, a step cap it could exceed, and numbers that belonged to another geometry — all in `src/tools/mmOptimizer.ts`** — Every entry below is a place where the field was reading less than the structure said, or reporting more than it had measured. The parameters themselves are unchanged wherever they were already right; what changes is what the run does with a structure that carries an amide, a sulfoxide, a terminal alkene, a lone ion or a hydrogen bond whose donor alone was selected.

  **A torsion no longer needs a heavy atom at both ends when the bond has a planarity term of its own** — the dihedral reader took the *first* neighbour that was not the bond's other end, so whether a torsion existed depended on the order the bonds happened to arrive in, and it then required that neighbour to be a heavy atom. A terminal =CH2 (propene), an amide N-H and an aniline -NH2 have nothing but hydrogens on that side, so their C=C, C-N and C-Ar dihedrals were dropped and the group was free to twist out of the plane it belongs in. The flanking atom is now chosen by a preference (a heavy neighbour always before a hydrogen, independent of the bond order) and a hydrogen is accepted when the bond carries a planarity term — double, aromatic or conjugated. A plain three-fold torsion with a hydrogen at one end is still dropped, so a methyl's rotation stays free, and a three-membered ring no longer builds a dihedral whose two flanks are its own third atom (a constant energy and a bogus term count).
