Verified (`verify_cavity.js`, 6.6–6.14 and the whole suite at **135 assertions**): a skewed cell (a = 1 Å, b = 6 Å, c with a 5.2 Å shear) at probe 1.0 marks every voxel the brute-force image walk marks and no voxel it does not, where the ±3 walk left a free voxel inside an atom's reach; a 2.5 Å cubic cell with a 5 Å probe reports zero free volume through the full-block path; and the analytic cell, cage, rock-salt, grid-cap and supercell checks are unchanged.

- **No two floating cards overlap, at any window size** — The placement pass used to take the first free slot on each card's edge in coarse steps with no height cap; a tall crystal card and a tall powder-XRD card could then be stepped past each other or run off the bottom of the viewport, and a card whose text changed height after the pass (the Edit Coordinates hint swaps between the long Alt-drag text and the short crystal one) kept a height budget sized for the old text until something else moved. The pass now **budgets before it places**: each side of the viewport gets a fair share of the width (a card wider than its budget is narrowed and its content wraps or scrolls), each column gets a height budget that shrinks its scrollable cards proportionally down to a 40 px floor (a card that genuinely cannot shrink is left alone and the others give way), and a failed attempt restores the card's own cap. Slots are scanned at **one pixel per step**, because two stacked cards can be left with only a couple of pixels of clearance. A card that fits nowhere at full width is retried at the widths that clear the placed cards, and as a last resort is capped to the container and given a free corner. Both changes that alter a card's height now re-run the pass — the Edit Coordinates hint swap and the XRD note a new radiation or range adds or drops — so a card is never laid out from a stale height. Verified on the **real page** in seven window sizes (1400×900 down to 560×540, **693 assertions**, `verify_panel_layout.js`): every visible pair of cards, chips and readouts is apart and inside the container. Every existing suite is unchanged and green (2 870 aromatic-dash, 257 CIF, 248 display-style, 237 ghost, 214 crystal, 192 toolbar, 187 crystal-export, 173 space-group, 159 ORCA, 137 crystal-edit, 117 XRD, 110 shortcuts, 101 clipboard, 80 view-card, 67 provenance, 55 VESTA, 54 neighbours, 53 lattice planes, 48 selection, 44 crystal-bonds, 44 autoplay, 17 polyhedra, 16 atom-instancing, 12 crystal-diff and 12 pick-style assertions).

- **A floating card now takes the width its content needs, not the width of its widest hidden row** — The Supercell Bounds card was **582 px wide** although every visible row in it is a label and a short input: its six action buttons are inline-level elements with `width:100%`, so the shrink-to-fit width pass measured all six side by side (their text adds up to about 600 px) even though they render stacked, and the card laid a 582 px sheet of dark background over the crystal it sits beside. The card is now a **fixed 210 px column**, the exact width of the **View** and **Edit Coordinates** cards that share its corner, so the right-hand column reads as one stack; the bounds are **three rows, one per axis**, each carrying its lower and upper input around a dash (the same six input IDs and the same `syncBndInputs` / change wiring, so the presets, Reset, Align Origin, undo and the supercell ceiling are untouched), and the spacing is tighter so the whole card — bounds, presets, cell parameters, disorder switch, lattice-plane calculator and all six buttons — fits its **424 px** budget without scrolling at 1400×900. The structure card at the top-left is capped at **min(300 px, width − 16 px)** so a long formula, space-group or amber warning line wraps instead of widening the card. Every other card was re-measured against its content and left as it was: they are already content-sized (powder XRD 456 px around its 440 px chart, cavities 392 px around its four-column table, interactions 330 px around its four checkboxes, View and Edit Coordinates 210, selection 280, rotate 320, MM 340, frequencies 300, route 360). Verified on the **real page** in seven window sizes (1400×900 down to 560×540): the bounds card renders at exactly 210 px with its content fitting at 1400×900, no pair of cards overlaps, and `verify_panel_layout.js` stays at **780 assertions**; the crystal (**223**), cell-edit (**137**), symmetry-check (**127**), lattice-plane (**53**), display-style (**260**), view-card (**80**), shortcuts (**115**) and cavity (**114**) suites are unchanged and green.

- **The VESTA reader uses the CIF reader's ruler, so one structure has one answer** — The reader kept private copies of the cell arithmetic and its tolerances, and folded images within 0.5 Å where the CIF reader folded only within 0.1 Å; the same structure could therefore come back with different atom counts depending on the extension, with split sites silently deleted. Both readers now share `crystalGeometry`'s expansion, grid and 0.1 Å coincidence rule; a VESTA file's SYMOP operations are kept as `crystal.symmetryOps` in CIF spelling (so a save or a diff knows how the cell was made) and STRUC is kept as `asymAtoms` with the operations that placed each site; every merge above round-off is reported, capped at six lines, and a file without a cell comes back empty with a warning instead of an invented 1 Å cube. Verified (`verify_vesta.js`, **55 assertions**): a VESTA cell written out as CIF and read back agrees atom for atom, the two readers give the same counts and positions, SYMOP survives, merges and split sites are reported, and an incomplete file warns.

- **A negative POSCAR scale is a target volume, and what the reader cannot use it now says** — A negative scale is VASP's other spelling: the target cell volume in Å³, reached by (V/V₀)^(1/3). The reader applied the number itself, which turned a requested 100 Å³ cell into a lattice mirrored through the origin and scaled by 100; it now computes the cube-root factor and applies it to Cartesian coordinates as well. A negative scale on a zero-volume cell warns and reads as 1.0 instead of dividing by zero. Selective-dynamics flags are kept on each atom, set out again on export (the `Selective dynamics` line is written only when some atom carries a flag, and an unflagged atom is free `T T T`), and the reader's other recoveries now travel as warnings. Verified in the crystal-export suite's POSCAR section (**42 assertions**, suite **187**): the cube-root volume and its Cartesian sites, the flags read, written and read back, the line present only when needed, and warnings for a zero-volume negative scale.

- **A crystal Diff compares the cells, so a file against itself is "Structures are identical"** — The left-hand structure is the **displayed supercell** (2352 atoms at 2×2×2, and even at 0..1 the molecules are drawn whole, outside the cell), while the file it was compared against is one **wrapped unit cell** (294 atoms). With more than one cell on screen the two lists do not even have the same atom count, so `findAtomMapping` answered **"Skeletons Differ"** before it looked at a graph; at 0..1 it could match, but every bond length and angle was then measured between an unwrapped molecule and a wrapped cell — two different drawings of the same object. A comparison with a crystal on either side now runs on what the two files actually state: the **six cell parameters** first (a/b/c within 0.01 Å, α/β/γ within 0.05°; a genuinely different cell is reported as **"Unit Cells Differ"** with both parameter sets, before any graph is read), then the **base cell's periodic graph** through the same skeleton matching on `baseAtoms` (a different graph is reported as "Skeletons Differ" with the base-cell counts). Bond lengths, angles and dihedrals are measured **through each bond's own periodic image**, so a bond across a cell face is its real length and a torsion along a periodic chain is the molecule's torsion, not the cell's — the same `computeConformationDiffs` loops and statistics run on a geometry adapter, so a crystal and a molecule cannot quietly disagree about what "bond length" means. The difference indices are base-cell indices and are translated to each half's display indices: on a supercell every image of a differing site wears the mark, the right-hand half marks the matching site, and the auto-mirror selection still pairs the two sides. The session survives an edit or a restyle (the cell comparison re-runs in place). Verified on the **real page** through the same `diffResult` message the extension sends (`verify_crystal_diff.js`, **12 assertions**): the same CIF, displayed as a 2×2×2 supercell, compared with itself is **"Structures are identical", 0 differences**, with the base cell's **340** periodic bonds in the statistics and no 2352-atom mapping anywhere; a cell 0.5 Å different is **"Unit Cells Differ"** with both a lengths, before any graph comparison. The right-hand cell travels in the payload as `baseAtoms` + lattice (the display-only atom list cannot state a cell), and the page detects its periodic bonds with the same generic detector the loaded cell uses (`detectCrystalBaseBondsFor`), which returns bonds instead of writing into `CRY`.

- **A site exactly on the upper cell face is drawn once, not twice** — The per-atom placement pass (periodic networks, isolated atoms) tested each image with a closed interval (`<= aMax`), so a site whose wrapped fractional coordinate is 0 appeared at both 0 and 1 in a 0..1 cell — the same atom drawn twice on the boundary. The whole-molecule pass has always been half-open on the upper face for exactly this reason; the per-atom pass now is too (`< aMax`), so a boundary site is one image, a 0..2 view holds two images of it, and a slab that contains neither image draws none. Verified by the crystal suite's fractional-boundary assertions (0.999 just below the face, exactly 0, exactly on the lower face of a 0.5..1.5 slab, and a slab with no image at all), and the mutation that closes the upper face again fails 7 assertions.

- **Every crystal export now describes one structure, and says which** — The four crystal formats disagreed about what they were writing. The POSCAR wrote the **displayed** atoms — a 2×2×2 view's 2352 atoms, their fractional coordinates between 0 and 2 — against the **unit cell's** lattice, so the atoms sat outside the box the file declared and the file described no structure at all; the CIF wrote the *symmetry-expanded* cell under the original space group and the original operation list, which expands a second time on the next read (a P2(1)/c file's 147 sites recovering as 294 atoms, and 294 again on the read after that) — the "expanded cell + full symop list" mixed body; the cube wrote a **negative** atom count (the cube convention for "orbital records follow", which readers then consume the grid as) and a 2×2×2 grid of zeros; and neither CIF nor VESTA wrapped a fractional coordinate, so an atom edited across a face was saved at 1.02. All four now write the **unit cell** — the `baseAtoms` the session edits and every rebuild redraws from — and declare it in the file. The **POSCAR** takes its atoms and its lattice from the same object, groups them by element and wraps each fraction into [0,1), and its comment line names the body (`unit cell, 294 atoms (not the displayed supercell)`), so a supercell view saves the cell it actually describes. The **CIF** declares `_symmetry_space_group_name_H-M 'P 1'` with the single identity operation and an `_audit_creation_method` line naming both the body and the group the symmetry came from, so reading it back and saving it again keeps the atom count; it wraps every fraction into [0,1) and writes `_cell_volume` (the lattice determinant), `_chemical_formula_sum` (the cell's composition in Hill order) and `_atom_site_site_symmetry_multiplicity` (1 per site under P1). The **VESTA** wraps the same way. The **cube** is a real cube — a positive atom count, which also states the units, so every coordinate is converted to Bohr instead of being written in Å under a header that says otherwise; the unit cell as the grid's box; and a grid of zeros whose size the dialog chooses (default **40** points per axis, capped at **64**; an oversized value is refused with the limit named rather than clamped, and no file is written); the second comment line says how it was built, so a consumer can tell a flat grid from a zero density. Verified against the **compiled page and parsers** (`verify_crystal_export.js`, **123 assertions**): a 2×2×2 view's POSCAR lists exactly the **4** cell sites with every fraction inside the declared box and reads back (`parseVasp`) at the same bond lengths, and the 294-site fixture at 2×2×2 saves **294** atoms against its own cell; a saved CIF carries the volume (`216.000000` on the 6 Å cell), the formula, P1 and the audit line, reads back **without warnings**, and **three save→read→save rounds keep 294 atoms and the same element counts**; the VESTA's every fraction is in [0,1); the cube's atom count is positive, its grid is 40×40×40 of zeros, its coordinates are Bohr (an atom at 1 Å reads back at 1 Å through `parseCube`), the grid follows the dialog (8 → 512 values) and 200 is refused. The existing suites are unchanged and green — 80 crystal, 44 crystal-bonds, 86 crystal-edit, 142 CIF (its save assertions rewritten for the P1 body: an expanded cell is declared P1 and keeps its original group in the audit line), 237 ghost, 157 display-style, 105 keyboard, 101 clipboard, 67 provenance, 48 selection, 2870 aromatic-dash, 159 ORCA, 44 autoplay, 192 toolbar, 12 pick-style, 80 view-card — and the client script re-parses.

- **Crystal bonds are perceived by the same module the molecular path runs, so a molecule in a cell gets the orders it gets as an XYZ** — the crystal path carried its own copy of the element tables (`CR2`/`BS`/`BC`) and decided each bond on its own, one bond at a time, which meant it had none of the shared perception module's passes: the order snap, the valence relaxation that pulls an over-coordinated centre back to a valence its element takes, the aromatic 1.5, the charge-aware solver. The same molecule drawn from a CIF and from an XYZ could therefore disagree about its own bonds — 142619's aromatic rings came out as alternating single and double bonds in the cell while the molecular path called them 1.5. The cell's atoms are now laid out the way the renderer draws them and handed to `detectBondsFromAtoms`, the perception module the host parses with and the viewport has always re-derived with: a finite molecule whole, at the same unwrap offsets `rebuildCrystal()` places it by (the walk is now one shared `crystalComponents()`, so the detector and the renderer cannot disagree about which atoms form a molecule), and a periodic network as the centre cell plus the neighbour images its split bonds point at — a cycle that comes back shifted has no consistent unwrap to lay out. Each bond the detector returns is written back as its **base pair plus the integer image it points at**, the form the renderer, the editors and the save path already read, one bond per base pair. Verified against the **compiled** page: CCDC 142619's **340** bonds carry exactly the orders its unwrapped molecular picture gets, bond for bond, aromatic 1.5 included; carbonate (C–O 1.28 Å), nitrate (N–O 1.25 Å) and carboxylate (C–O 1.25/1.33 Å) give the same bond set and the same orders through a cell as on their own, at their real lengths, including when the ion straddles a face and the file has wrapped an oxygen into the next cell; a chain wrapped across a face keeps both of its bonds with the unwrapped molecule's orders; a polymer chain keeps the centre-cell-plus-image cut the renderer draws and all three of its bonds.

- **The minimum image is enumerated instead of rounded, so a skewed cell no longer loses a bond or draws one a cell long** — `Math.round(Δfrac)` answers "which image is nearest" only for an orthogonal cell. In a triclinic one it can leave a bond a whole cell long (the two atoms drawn straight through the structure) or pick an image that is not the nearest, which loses the bond altogether: over **1000 random triclinic cells** it disagrees with a brute-force search in **407**, and **21** of the 127 pairs that really bond would have been missed. The search now enumerates the shifts a cell can possibly need and takes the shortest, sized exactly rather than guessed: a repeat `s_i` moves a displacement only across the planes the other two lattice vectors span, at a fixed spacing `w_i`, so an image is at least `|s_i − f_i| · w_i` away — every image inside a cutoff `c` lies in `s_i ∈ [⌈f_i − n_i⌉, ⌊f_i + n_i⌋]` with `n_i = c / w_i`. An ordinary cell needs `n_i = 1`, which is the classic block of 27 images; a cell whose planes are narrower than the cutoff widens it to 5×5×5 and beyond (capped at four repeats, so a degenerate cell cannot explode). A displacement exactly midway between two cells is a tie, and the walk runs from the largest repeat down so a tie keeps the answer rounding gave — an orthogonal cell answers exactly as it did before. The free-direction probe behind Add Atom (`crystalImageDirection`) reads the same search, so a substituent added to a molecule that straddles a face points away from its neighbour's image in a skewed cell too. The pair loop also stopped asking the table — which normalises two element strings — for every one of its n²/2 pairs; it reads a per-atom widest cutoff and asks for the exact pair cutoff only on the pairs that survive, so the 6672-atom POSCAR fixture is perceived once in **0.4 s**.

  Verified against the **compiled** page — the enumeration, the pair search and the component walk cut out of it and driven against independent references — a new suite (`.workbuddy/tools/verify_crystal_bonds.js`, **44 assertions**) plus the two existing crystal suites, unchanged (**80** and **86**). The **1000-cell sweep** (lengths 2.8–12 Å, angles 55–125°, displacements within ±0.6 of a cell) matches a brute force over a 13×13×13 block in the shift **and** the distance in every case, and the enumeration answers every one of the 127 bonded cases while rounding loses 21 of them; a 2 Å repeat with a 3 Å cutoff reaches the second image; 142619 gives the same bond set and orders as its unwrapped molecular picture; the 6672-atom POSCAR is perceived in 0.4 s. **Four mutations** — the minimum image rounded again (8 assertions fail), a finite molecule not unwrapped (17), a periodic network given no images (6), the perceived order not read back (7) — are each caught, so none of the checks is vacuous. Every other suite is unchanged and green: 2 870 aromatic-dash, 140 CIF, 237 ghost, 157 display-style, 105 keyboard, 101 clipboard, 67 provenance, 48 selection, 192 toolbar, 44 autoplay, 159 ORCA, 80 view-card and 12 pick-style assertions.

- **A coordinate edit made on a crystal's picture is now written back into the cell the file keeps** — Bond Length, Bond Angle, Dihedral and Rotate Group moved the **displayed** atoms and nothing else, while a crystal saves its base cell and every `rebuildCrystal()` (a bounds change, an undo, a deleted atom) redraws from that cell — so an edit was lost twice: a Save As to CIF or VESTA wrote the old geometry, and the next rebuild dropped it from the screen as well, while POSCAR/cube (which write the displayed atoms) kept it. The displayed picture was never the structure a crystal saves, so the two formats of one session could disagree about it. Every image the builder places now records the **integer cell offset** it sits at relative to its base site (`imgShift`, one line per placement pass), and a new `commitCrystalCoords()` turns the move of any image back into the move of its site: `delta_frac = frac(image) − frac(base) − imgShift`, measured with the same inverse lattice the builder used. The dialogs commit at **OK** — a preview still shows on screen before that and a Cancel still puts everything back — and Rotate Group commits at each committed angle and when the session ends (Done, Clear, a mode switch), so the angle left on screen is the angle in the file. A site drawn many times (a 2×2×2 supercell shows each site eight times) takes the displacement of its **most-moved image**, applied once: a sum would multiply the edit by the number of copies, and any other image would lose most of it. A **bond drawn as a stub across a cell face** is refused by the Bond Length / Bond Angle / Dihedral dialogs with a message — the two atoms on screen are a whole cell apart on purpose, so the distance between them is not the bond length; growing the supercell and picking an image with both ends present is the way to edit it. A bond-order change made in the Bond Length dialog reaches the base bond list too (a bond removed there stays removed), and Cancel puts the cell back from the undo snapshot the dialog opened with — including a bond the dialog had invented. Verified against the **compiled** page with **86 assertions**: the edited length reaches the base cell and comes back out of a CIF written by the shipping Save dialog (1.30 Å read back), a 2×2×2 edit moves the site once rather than eight times, a rotated image keeps the internal geometry through the write-back, the preview leaves the saved cell untouched until OK, one Undo restores the length the cell had, and the create/remove/cancel paths keep the picture and the cell in step.

- **A crystal's undo snapshot is the cell, not the supercell** — `pushUndo()` deep-copied the whole displayed supercell *plus* the base atoms for every crystal edit, so the larger the supercell on screen the more expensive each snapshot was, with up to 50 of them held. A crystal's picture is a pure function of three things — the base cell, its bonds and the bounds — so those three are now the snapshot, and `doUndo()` rebuilds the picture from them. A 2×2×2 view now snapshots exactly the bytes of a 1×1×1 one (measured on CCDC 142619: 124 KB either way, against 519 KB for the 2×2×2 *display* alone) and `pushUndo()` costs the same on both; undo also restores the **supercell bounds** the snapshot was taken under (the six inputs follow), so the picture it rebuilds is the one the edit was made in and the selection's indices stay valid. The molecular path is untouched: a non-crystal structure still snapshots its atoms and bonds. Verified with 15 assertions across both paths: the snapshot carries no displayed atoms, equal bytes at 1×1×1 and 2×2×2, one undo takes an edit out of the cell and redraws the cell view, and a bounds change made before the undo is reverted with the edit.

- **An atom added to a crystal now obeys the cell it is added to** — The Add Atom dialog placed the new site at `anchor + dir × length` and pushed the bond with no periodic shift, so in a small cell the site could land outside [0,1) (the file kept that coordinate while the picture wrapped it), and an anchor near a face could be drawn as one long bond straight across the cell. The new site is now **wrapped into the cell** in fractional coordinates, and the bond records the **integer image** it points at (`shift`, computed from the cell offset between the anchor and the placed position), so the displayed bond is either a normal bond inside one image or a stub into the neighbouring cell — never a long bond through the cell. The free direction for the new atom is read through the **bonded images** as well (each base bond's shift is honoured), so a molecule that straddles a face no longer points a new substituent through the wall. Verified against the compiled page: a site added at 0.98 of the cell wraps to 0.16, its bond carries shift (0,0,1), the bond length through that image is exactly the requested 1.09 Å and no unshifted bond on screen spans the cell; with a bonded anchor the new atom points away from the neighbour's image.

  The three changes are guarded by a new suite (`.workbuddy/tools/verify_crystal_edit.js`, **86 assertions**) with five mutations — the image offset dropped from the whole-molecule pass (16 failures), the write-back taking the least-moved image instead of the most-moved (1), the added site's shift zeroed (5), the display atoms put back into the crystal snapshot (5), the bounds left unrestored on undo (3) — every one caught. The existing suites are unchanged and green: 80 crystal, 140 CIF, 237 ghost, 157 display-style, 105 keyboard, 101 clipboard, 67 provenance, 48 selection, 2 870 aromatic-dash, 192 toolbar, 44 autoplay, 159 ORCA, 80 view-card and 12 pick-style assertions, and the client script re-parses (9 743 lines).

- **A CIF's symmetry was read from one of three dictionary generations, and the other two loaded as P1 without a word** — The reader looked for `_symmetry_equiv_pos_as_xyz` and for nothing else. The core dictionary (CIF 1.2) writes the same list as `_space_group_symop_operation_xyz`, CIF 2 writes `_space_group_symop.operation_xyz` (the data name is split by a dot, not an underscore), and the space-group name has the same three spellings — so a file written under any of the newer ones reached the expander with **one operation, the identity, and no warning at all**, and was drawn as P1: a two-site structure that states two operations came out as two atoms instead of four, with nothing on screen saying the cell was half empty. Lookups now go through a **normalised tag** (`_space_group_symop.operation_xyz` and `_space_group_symop_operation_xyz` are one name, and the same rule makes a CIF 2 file's `_atom_site.fract_x` the same column as `_atom_site_fract_x`) and a priority list per concept, and they are exact instead of substring matches — `_geom_bond_atom_site_label_1` can no longer answer a question about `_atom_site_label`, nor `_atom_site_aniso_label` one about `label`. Operations are read from a loop, from a one-row item, or from a semicolon text block, and a file that lists the same operation twice now expands it once. **What cannot be read is said**: a file with no operation tag ends in the structure panel as `no symmetry operations in the file: read as P1 (declared space group P -1)`, and a tag whose contents make no sense as `no readable symmetry operation under _space_group_symop.operation_xyz: read as P1`. A bare `_key` followed by a `;` block — how a multi-line space group or systematic name is spelled — used to be tokenised and then dropped, leaving the item empty; the block is now attached to the name above it. Verified by writing the same structure under all three generations and comparing the cells atom for atom to nine decimals (identical), by the CCDC 142619 fixture (294 atoms, C138 H116 N14 O26, unchanged), and by 140 assertions with 20 mutations, every one of which the suite catches.

- **A CIF number, an element symbol and a `?` were taken at face value** — `parseNumber` called `parseFloat` on whatever it was handed, so a file without `_cell_length_a` threw `Cannot read properties of undefined` out of the parser instead of loading; `?` was a value like any other, which is how a file whose `_symmetry_space_group_name_H-M` is `?` came to show `SG: ?` in the panel, write `'?'` back into a saved CIF and use a question mark as its title; and an oxidation state stayed in the element name, so `Fe3+` became an element called "Fe3+" and missed every table the viewer has — radius, colour, valence, mass. Now a non-string is a missing field; the number ends at the first `(` of `10.7307(7)`, which also survives an uncertainty that lost its closing bracket where deleting brackets would have glued the digits on; `?` and `.` are absent everywhere, so a missing space group is not a group and is not written back; the element is the leading run of letters with the state removed (`Fe3+` → `Fe`, `O2-` → `O`, `Cu(II)` → `Cu`, `N+` → `N`); and an incomplete unit cell returns an **empty structure with a warning** instead of a 1 Å cube, which is what `parseCif('data_x\n')` used to throw on. A missing cell angle still defaults to 90°, but now says so. Verified: `parseCif('data_x\n')` returns no atoms and one warning; five oxidation-state spellings come out as their elements; `?` reaches neither the panel nor a save.

- **Two CIF sites 0.3 Å apart were folded into one atom** — Symmetry images were deduplicated at a 0.5 Å radius (`dx² + dy² + dz² < 0.25`), a full bond length's worth of slack. That is far more than rounding needs: the only images that must coincide are a site's own images on a special position, and they land on each other to within 1e-9 Å — so the tolerance was really deciding which *distinct* sites the file lists would survive, and a split or partially occupied pair 0.3 Å apart came out as a single atom. The radius is **0.1 Å** now, and a merge above 1e-4 Å — anything that is not an exact image — is **reported**: `site C2: an image 0.050 A from an existing atom was merged into it`, capped at six plus a count. Folding an exact image onto a special position stays silent: that is what symmetry does, and a warning per special position would drown the cases worth reading. The dedupe itself moved from an all-pairs scan to a **grid** one tolerance wide, because expansion compares every image against every atom placed so far and that is quadratic in a framework's atom count: 2000 sites × 24 operations (7 987 atoms) now parse in **19 ms** end to end, against **184 ms** for the all-pairs dedupe alone on the same images. Verified: sites 0.30 Å apart stay two atoms, 0.099 Å merges and 0.101 Å does not, and 142619 is unchanged at 294 atoms with the same element counts.

## [1.0.3] - 2026-10-27

### Added

- **A display style for the view — ball and stick, space-filling or stick — that does not fall back when the toolbar moves on** — The View card gains a **Display** selector with three models: **Ball & Stick** (the drawing this viewer has always produced, and the default), **Space-filling** (each atom as its van der Waals sphere, no sticks) and **Stick** (the sticks stay and every sphere shrinks to the thickness of the sticks that meet at it, so the model reads as tubes with no beads and no notch at a bend). The choice is a property of the **view**, not of a mode: it is one variable above every block that reads it, and no mode switch, frame, molecule, undo or edit resets it — leave View for Bond Length, Move Atoms or MM, or switch a trajectory's frame, and the structure is still drawn the way it was chosen. The View card is only where the style is *changed*; the control leaves with View, the style does not.

  **One rule, three readers, so the picture cannot be half one style and half another.** The atom radius (`drawnRadius`) is the single answer to "how big is this atom": ball returns `atomRadius` unchanged — the default drawing is literally the old expression, not a copy of it — a space-filling sphere is the element's van der Waals radius (the table the host resolves a script's `Surf` probe against, pinned element for element), and a stick joint is the thickness of the thickest stick meeting at that atom, read off the bond list in one pass, so a script that draws thin lines does not grow beads and an isolated ion does not vanish (it keeps the default joint). A ghost stays the small marker it is in all three styles: it is a point without a nucleus, so neither a van der Waals sphere nor a joint is a meaningful size for it. **A space-filling model draws no sticks** — the decision lives in `createBond`, the one door every piece is declared through, so the scene build, a moved region's rebuild and both halves of a comparison obey it without being told again; **a stick model changes only the spheres**, and every piece keeps the transform it already had. A VMD script's per-atom sphere scale is applied in the ball style (that is what it is for) and deliberately not in the others: it is a ratio inside a ball-and-stick recipe, and a zero there means "this representation draws no sphere", which in a space-filling model would empty the picture; the script's colours and surfaces still apply. A comparison is restyled whole: Diff draws its right half from its own model, so a style change rebuilds that half too rather than leaving one structure in one model and the other in another — in place, so the two view states, the camera and the pan stay where the user put them, and carrying the difference marks and the right-hand selection across the rebuild instead of dropping them.

  **A joint is not something a hand can aim at, so the stick model gets a pick target of its own.** Picking is how every measurement, selection and edit starts, and a stick joint is deliberately thin: on a 24 Å structure a 0.24 Å joint is under two pixels across, and **only 4 of its 12 atoms could be pointed at at all** (measured in a real engine, sweeping the pointer over the structure). A press or a hover that misses every sphere is therefore re-tested — **in the stick style only** — against a cone around each atom whose radius is fixed in **pixels**, so the pointer needs the same precision at every zoom level and the nearest atom along the ray wins. The default balls and the space-filling spheres are their own targets and are **untouched**: the same page with the rule disabled reaches exactly the same atoms at exactly the same points, count for count. The hover tooltip also stops carrying its own copy of "which atom is under the cursor" — it asks the same `getClickedAtom` a press does, so the two can never disagree and the wider target applies to both. A change of style re-takes the panel layout as well, because the panels keep clear of the box the *drawn* spheres occupy and a space-filling model is several times the footprint of the balls it replaced.

  Verified against the **compiled** client — the real `drawnRadius`, `stickJointRadii`, `rebuildScene`, `rebuildBondPieces`/`createBond`, `buildDiffModel`/`rebuildDiffModel` and `setDisplayStyle`, cut out of the page and driven against stub meshes, a stub bond pool and a stub select — **157 assertions**: the default style compared against the old expression element for element and piece for piece (every transform, not just the counts), a space-filling scene at the tabulated radii with no pieces at all, a stick scene whose joints follow the sticks, a value the page does not know refused (with the control put back to the truth), a repeated choice rebuilding nothing, a change of style in Diff mode reaching the right half and keeping its marks, and the page's van der Waals table compared against the host's export. **14 mutations** — the viewer opening on another style (36 assertions), space-filling falling back to the ball radius (11), a joint drawn at the ball radius (9), the thinnest stick deciding the joint (1), sticks still drawn in a space-filling model (4), one radius changed in the page's table (6), a repeated choice rebuilding anyway (5), a refused value written to the state (11), the comparison ignoring the style (4), the comparison keeping its sticks (2), a restyle dropping the marks (1), the right half never restyled (1), a ghost given a van der Waals size (4), the scene build still asking for the ball radius (8) — are each caught, so none of the above is vacuous. **12 assertions** in a real engine pin pointer reachability (every atom of a 12-membered ring reachable in all three styles, a click selecting exactly what the hover resolved at that point, and the same page run against a build with the rule stripped: ball and space-filling identical point for point, stick down to 4 of 12). **80 layout assertions** in headless Edge (four window sizes down to 380 px) pin the control inside the 210 px card: full content width, no clipped option, the reading order Auto Rotate → hint → Display → select, the card inside the window and shorter than the view, and a chosen value that moves neither the card's height nor the control's width. The existing suites are unchanged and green: 2 870 aromatic-dash, 237 ghost, 101 clipboard, 105 keyboard, 67 provenance, 48 selection, 44 autoplay and 159 ORCA/trajectory assertions.

- **Ghost atoms — a position with no nucleus, and every format that can say so** — An atom can now be a **ghost**, which is what a counterpoise (BSSE) calculation and a geometry definition both need: a point that keeps the element it stands for but has no nucleus, no electrons and no bonds. **Add Atom** gains a *Ghost atom* switch and an `X (dummy point)` element, and **Replace Atom** gains a *Ghost* switch that converts the whole selection into ghosts **of their own elements** in one step — the gesture a counterpoise setup actually needs (select one monomer, make it ghosts, save the Gaussian input), and the only reading under which a mixed selection means anything; the switch is pre-ticked when everything selected is already a ghost, so pressing Replace is never a silent un-ghosting. A ghost keeps its element (`C-Bq` in a Gaussian input, `C:` in an ORCA one) or is a bare dummy (`X`), is drawn as a small faint grey-blue marker whatever it stands for, and is labelled `C*3` / `X5` everywhere the page names an atom — the tooltip says `(ghost)`.

  **A ghost is excluded from everything that needs a nucleus, at one predicate.** `isGhostAtom` (the `ghost` flag *or* the dummy element `X`, both spellings meaning the same thing) decides drawing, colour, formula, electron count, label and every bond guard, so a structure cannot have a ghost that one pass treats as an atom and another does not. **Bond perception never bonds it**: the connectivity pass leaves a ghost out of the spatial grid and normalises it to a dummy for the valence passes, so a ghost laid exactly on top of a real carbon adds no sticks, does not perturb one real bond (the derived bond list is compared against the ghost-free structure's, bond for bond), and does not send the valence solver after a component it could never fix. **A bond that touches a ghost is dropped on load** — a PDB `CONECT`, a MOL2 bond block or a Gaussian `connect` section may name one, and `ensureBonds` is the single door every load path passes through. The **formula and the electron count** leave ghosts out and the info panel lists them separately (`Atoms: 4 (2 ghosts)`, `Ghosts: 2 (no bonds, no basis, not in the formula)`); **Add H** and **Molecular Mechanics** skip them, a ghost is never in the mobile set, and its Lennard-Jones well depth is zero — so a ghost parked 1 Å from a carbon cannot drag it (without that, the same run throws the carbon 2.4 Å); the **Bond Length** dialog refuses to create a bond to one and puts its order control back to None. Turning a ghost back into a real atom re-derives **that atom's** bonds from the geometry — the same detector a load uses, and only bonds that do not exist yet are added, so nothing else is rewritten — in the same undo step. Ghost atoms are refused for **crystal structures** with a message: CIF, POSCAR, VESTA and cube have no way to say "this site has no nucleus", so a ghost written to one would come back as a real atom.

  **Every format writes its own spelling, and reads all of them back.** Gaussian `C-Bq` / `Bq`, ORCA `C:` / `X`, XYZ `X`, PDB `X` with a `HETATM` record and a `DUM` residue, MOL2 `Du`, MDL Mol `*`, TURBOMOLE `$coord` `q`, MOPAC `XX`; the parsers recognise all of these plus `Xx`, `DA`, `Dummy` and `(Bq)`. The formats with no ghost concept are given the **dummy** spelling rather than the atom's, because a ghost must never come back as a real atom, and the Save dialog says which is which before the file is written. A structure **without** ghosts saves byte-identically to before (pinned, format by format), and the provenance line records the change in words (`1 ghost atom added`, `2 atoms made ghosts`, `1 ghost made real`) so the file says what was done to it.

  Verified against the **compiled** extension and the **built page** — the host parsers, the bond detector, the force field and (cut out of the page, the way the clipboard suite does it) `isGhostAtom`, `formulaFromAtoms`, `updateMolInfo`, `atomRadius`, `doSave`, both atom dialogs and the clipboard — **237 assertions**: every spelling read and no real atom misread (`C`, `Cl`, `Xe`, `C-CT`, `O-O--0.5`), the same structure with and without a ghost deriving identical bonds on both the host and the evaluated `BOND_PERCEPTION_SOURCE`, **every written format read back** (a saved ghost returns as one, a saved atom as one, the ghost's element survives where the format carries it, and only the real bonds come back), the ghost-free text of every format compared against the old output, the refusals (a crystal, a bond order on a ghost pair, a ghost anchor), the dialog note, the pre-ticked Replace switch, the re-bond of a converted atom, and the clipboard carrying the flag while dropping a stored bond into a ghost. **Three mutations** — the ghost predicate returning false, the XYZ writer losing the dummy spelling, the ghost's well depth restored — fail exactly the matching assertions (4, 1 and 3), so none of them is vacuous. The existing suites are unchanged and green: 2 870 aromatic-dash, 101 clipboard, 67 provenance, 100 keyboard, 48 selection, 44 autoplay and 159 ORCA/trajectory assertions.

- **Esc, Ctrl+Z and Ctrl+S — the three keys a hand reaches for before the mouse** — The page's keyboard now has one door: these three are settled at the top of it, before the viewer's own keys (arrows, Delete) are read, so a shortcut and an existing control can never both answer the same press. The modifier is **Ctrl on Windows/Linux and Cmd on macOS**, and each shortcut answers to the **key** *or* the **physical code**, so it still fires on a layout that reports a different character — an AZERTY Ctrl+Z arrives as `z`, a Cyrillic one as `я`, and both are the one press the user made. **Esc backs out of exactly one level**, in the order the states were built up: it presses the open dialog's own **Cancel** (the path that knows what cancelling means — several of them put back the geometry they were previewing — with a plain hide only as the fallback for a dialog that has no cancel button), else **abandons a box-select rectangle** still being dragged (it has selected nothing yet), else **leaves the current tool for View**, else **lets go of the selection**. Every step takes back something the user started and nothing else, so a second press can only undo a smaller thing and can never touch the structure; and Esc is the one key that also works from inside a dialog's text field, because that is how a user gets out of it. **Ctrl+Z is the Undo button**, and it refuses two states where an undo would do damage rather than work: while a **dialog is open** (it previews geometry on top of an undo step of its own — an undo behind its back would split the two apart, and its Cancel would then pop someone else's step) and inside a **text field**, where Ctrl+Z stays the browser's own text undo. **Ctrl+S opens the same Save dialog as the Save As button**, and with that dialog already up it **presses its Save button**, keeping the format already chosen — so "Ctrl+S, pick a format, Ctrl+S" saves without the mouse. The three also decline what is not theirs: no Shift (Ctrl+Shift+Z is redo elsewhere and there is no redo here), no Alt (AltGr sends Ctrl+Alt), no stacking a dialog on a dialog. Nothing is contributed to the editor's keybindings, so no VS Code or Trae shortcut is taken over, and the keys that were already there keep their meanings untouched — arrows still rotate and spin, Delete still deletes, Ctrl+arrow is still fragment rotation, and typing in a field is still the field's. The Undo and Save As buttons now say their own keys in their tooltips (`Ctrl+Z` / `Cmd+Z`). Verified by cutting the keyboard block out of the **compiled** client and driving it in a stub DOM with **63 assertions**, one per real key event and its focus: Esc's four levels and its no-op case (which must leave the default to the host), a dialog cancelled from inside its own field, Ctrl+Z refused behind a dialog and inside a field, Cmd on macOS, the AZERTY and Cyrillic presses both reaching the same action, Ctrl+S opening the dialog and confirming it, Ctrl+S opening nothing on top of another dialog and not firing from a field, Ctrl+Shift+Z, Ctrl+Alt+Z and Ctrl+Shift+S all left alone, and the pre-existing arrow / arrow-in-a-field / Delete / Ctrl+arrow behaviour unchanged — plus the integration side a stub cannot see: every name the handler calls is one the client really declares, the listener is registered **exactly once** on the document (a second door would make each shortcut answer twice), and the block sits inside the page's own IIFE. Five mutations — Esc handled after the field guard, Ctrl+Z let through behind a dialog, Ctrl+S no longer confirming, Esc skipping the tool level, Ctrl+S no longer checking the field — each fail exactly the matching assertions (10, 1, 2, 1 and 1), so none of them is vacuous; the whole page's client script is re-parsed after the edit to catch a template-level syntax slip.

- **Ctrl+C / Ctrl+X / Ctrl+V — a clipboard for the structure itself** — A **fragment** can now be copied out of the view and pasted back into it, which is what "duplicate this ligand" or "put that group over there" actually needs. **Copy** takes the selected atoms and **only the bonds among them** — re-indexed into the fragment, bond orders included — and stores the fragment **relative to its own centroid**, so the payload holds no position at all: a paste is then a translation, and the same fragment can be pasted again and again. **Cut** is copy plus **the same batch deletion the toolbar performs** (so a crystal loses its base atoms and base bonds exactly as a plain Delete does), with **one** undo step for the pair, and the status line says how to get the fragment back. **Paste** appends the fragment **beside** the structure, at the **very spot an import would choose**: the bounding-sphere rule was lifted out of `importStructure` into `placeBlockBeside`, which both paths now call, so the two placements cannot drift apart and the import's own numbers are unchanged (closest atoms 7 Å apart). A paste **selects what arrived**, so it can be moved, cut or copied straight away, zooms out when the copy lands outside the view, and is **one** undo step. **No bond is invented between the copy and its new neighbour**: a paste is not a merge, so unlike Import it does not re-detect the combined system, and the fragment keeps exactly the orders it was copied with. Every refusal is explicit and leaves the structure untouched — **paste on a crystal** (a cell's contents are regenerated from its base atoms, so the copy would vanish on the next rebuild), **cut and paste in Diff mode**, **cut and paste behind a dialog** (which is previewing geometry on top of an undo step of its own), a paste that would pass **20 000 atoms**, and an empty clipboard. Copy is read-only, so it works everywhere, dialogs and comparisons included. The clipboard is the **viewer's own**, not the system one: nothing you copied in another application is replaced, and nothing is ever read back from it. A copy is mirrored into the panel's **localStorage** on a best-effort basis, so a fragment can be pasted into another panel; whatever comes back is re-validated first (a real element symbol, finite coordinates, bond indices in range, size under the limit) and **discarded whole** rather than half-used if it is not a fragment — and with storage denied, copy and paste still work inside the panel. On the keyboard side the three keys obey the rules the other shortcuts set: they are ignored inside a text field (that Ctrl+C/Ctrl+V belongs to the field) and **ignored on key auto-repeat** (a held Ctrl+V would otherwise paste the fragment over and over), cut and paste wait for a closed dialog; copy is unconditional, because the page carries no selectable text of its own (see Fixed). Like the rest, they answer to `e.key` or `e.code` and to Ctrl or Cmd. Saved titles say what happened to the geometry ("5 atoms cut", "5 atoms pasted") through the same provenance clauses as every other edit. Verified against the **compiled** client, with the placement and clipboard code cut out of the page and driven against a stub structure — **101 assertions**: the fragment carries the selection's atoms, only the bonds inside it and their orders; the copy is centroid-relative and leaves the structure and the undo stack untouched; a paste grows the structure, appends the fragment's bond at the new indices, invents none across the gap, selects what arrived, records its clause and pushes exactly one snapshot; two pastes in a row do not land on each other; a paste into an empty structure is centred on the origin; a cut deletes exactly the selection through the toolbar path, keeps the fragment and reports 2 atoms cut; a non-finite coordinate, an empty fragment, a malformed element symbol, an out-of-range bond and an oversized payload are each rejected or trimmed; a fragment still pastes after another panel copied it, impossible bonds from storage are dropped while the atoms are kept, and storage that throws stops neither action; and the **import still lands exactly where it did** — same translation, same source clause, same single snapshot — which is what sharing the placement had to preserve. The keyboard harness grows to **90 assertions** (the previous 63 plus the new keys): Ctrl+C copies and consumes the key, does not copy on a text selection or on repeat, and works behind a dialog; Ctrl+X waits for a closed dialog and still cuts when text is selected; Ctrl+V refuses behind a dialog, in a text field, on repeat and with Shift, and fires on Cmd and on a Cyrillic layout. **13 mutations** — the fragment left un-centred, bonds taken from outside the selection, paste not an undo step, the separation buffer zeroed, cut not deleting, paste allowed on a crystal, stored coordinates unvalidated, bonds not offset into the extended list, the import translation dropped, the import's source clause dropped, paste repeating on a held key, copy taking over a text selection, paste growing behind a dialog — each fail exactly the matching assertions (2, 6, 2, 4, 5, 3, 2, 1, 2, 1, 1, 2 and 2), so none of them is vacuous; the page's client script is re-parsed after the edit, and the trajectory auto-play (**44**) and save-provenance (**65**) suites still pass unchanged.

- **Ctrl+A selects the whole structure — and the highlight pass behind it stopped walking the selection once per atom** — **Select All** does what dragging a box over the entire view does: every atom on screen, which in Diff mode means both halves. It changes no coordinate, so it is allowed in every mode and takes no undo step; it refuses only behind an **open dialog**, whose preview was captured for the selection it opened with, and it **ends a move/rotate session in flight first** — exactly as releasing the mouse would — rather than handing a drag that is halfway through a different atom set. Where a mode already words a finished selection (**Select Atoms**, **Replace Atom**, **Add H**, MM) it keeps that wording; a measurement mode is deliberately **not** asked to finish the selection, so Ctrl+A on a two-atom structure cannot pop the Bond Length dialog by itself — it selects, and the next click starts the measurement as usual. The key is consumed even when the action is refused, because handing it back to the browser would select the page's text instead, and it is ignored on **auto-repeat**, which would otherwise rebuild the whole selection and repaint every atom on each repeat. A text field keeps its own select-all.

  The highlight pass Select All depends on (`highlightSelected`) was rewritten from **one `indexOf` per mesh** to **one lookup for the pass**. With the whole structure selected the old form was quadratic — **135 ms at 20 000 atoms**, paid on every click that repainted the selection — and it is now **0.5 ms**. The answer is unchanged, and that is what the verification pins: the new expression is compared against the old one over **200 randomised cases** (random selections, repeated indices, indices past the end of the structure, diff-highlighted meshes) for **every** mesh, with zero mismatches. Verified against the **compiled** client, the two functions cut out of the page and driven against stub meshes — **40 assertions**: every atom selected and named, the one-atom wording, an empty structure refused without touching the selection, a dialog refused with its selection baseline intact, both halves in Diff mode through the two-sided line, the mode-specific wording where a mode has one, the measurement mode that must not be asked to finish, a drag ended before the selection changes, a supercell selected atom by atom, the highlight levels themselves (a selected atom outranks a diff highlight, a stale index highlights nothing, the right-hand pass runs only inside Diff mode, a redraw is requested) and the equivalence sweep. The keyboard harness grows to **100 assertions** (Ctrl+A routed and consumed, not taken from a text field, ignored on repeat, consumed even when refused behind a dialog, Shift not claimed, Cmd and a Cyrillic layout both reaching it). **8 mutations** — Select All allowed behind a dialog, the Diff right side left unselected, every mode asked to finish the selection, the highlight lookup off by one, the Diff side not highlighted, the drag not ended first, Ctrl+A repeating on a held key, the refusal handing the key back to the page — each fail exactly the matching assertions (4, 1, 3, 4, 1, 1, 1 and 2), so none of them is vacuous; the page's client script is re-parsed after the edit (8136 lines).

- **ORCA optimization and frequency runs now drive the same panels a Gaussian log does** — ORCA writes the same facts as Gaussian in a different shape, and the convergence chart, the normal-mode playback and the route panel were written against the Gaussian shape, so an ORCA `.out` used to open as a bare trajectory: geometries, nothing else. It is now read into the shape those panels expect, in **one pass** over the file, with every block recognised by its own header line and every block parser handing back the index of the first line it did not use — so no block is scanned twice and no header is seen from inside another block. **Geometry** comes from the `CARTESIAN COORDINATES (ANGSTROEM)` blocks, labelled by the banner that precedes each one (`Cycle 1` … `Cycle 4`, then `Final` for the geometry the run actually converged to), with the `(A.U.)` blocks parsed as the fallback for a run that suppressed the Ångström ones through `%output`. **Energies** are `FINAL SINGLE POINT ENERGY` (and the correlated methods' `Total Energy : … Eh`); because ORCA prints a cycle's geometry *before* the energy computed at it, the energy is bound back to the frame still waiting for one, which is what lets the chart highlight the point belonging to the structure on screen. **The convergence table** (`|Geometry convergence|`) becomes the step series: `MAX`/`RMS gradient` are the force curves, `MAX`/`RMS step` the displacement curves, and each step's energy is the one printed for the geometry it relaxes — its `Energy change` row is a difference and is deliberately not stored as an energy. **The threshold lines are the run's own**: ORCA states its criteria in the `Convergence Tolerances` block (`TolMAXG`/`TolRMSG`/`TolMAXD`/`TolRMSD`), so a run told to converge tighter than the default now shows the line it was actually held to, and when that block is not printed the same numbers are recovered from the table's own `Tolerance` column. **Frequencies**: ORCA prints all 3N modes, the first 3N−6 of which are the translations and rotations listed as `0.00 cm**-1`; those are dropped, so the list matches the 3N−6 (3N−5) entries a Gaussian log gives — a mode is dropped only when its frequency **and** its displacement vector are zero, so a genuinely soft mode that rounds to `0.00` survives — while imaginary modes keep the negative sign ORCA prints them with, which the panel already marks with `*`. The `NORMAL MODES` matrix is read panel by panel (a header of integers introduces the modes, each row is a degree of freedom), grouped into per-atom vectors once the run's atom count is settled, and the `IR SPECTRUM` intensities are attached per mode in km/mol — the same unit as Gaussian's `IR Inten`. **The Route panel** shows ORCA's `!` simple-input line, recovered from the `INPUT FILE` block the output echoes, one keyword per token, with the `opt` badge on runs that optimize; **charge and multiplicity** come from the same echo (`* xyz <c> <m>`) and from the `SCF SETTINGS` block, and are filled in on frames created before either was printed. Verified against the reference `test/orca_test/h2o.out` (ORCA 6.1.1, `! B3LYP def2-SVP Opt freq`) through the **compiled** parser — **159 assertions**: the five geometries with their own energies, the four steps with the energy of the geometry each relaxes, the first cycle's four-row table (it prints no `Energy change`) and the last cycle's five-row one, the three real frequencies with their IR intensities and the direction of each normal mode (the bend moving both hydrogens in opposite `y`, the symmetric stretch likewise, the asymmetric one along `z`), the charge/multiplicity, the route and its `opt` badge, the tolerances from both the block and the table column, and the whole thing again through `parseLogFile`. **Robustness** is pinned by construction rather than by inspection: an Ångström-suppressed run reads its geometry from the Bohr blocks (and lands on the same structure), a file truncated mid-table or mid-matrix still yields every geometry it has, a file cut inside a coordinate block drops that one frame instead of showing a molecule with its last atoms missing (the atom count every other block agrees on decides), a file cut inside the displacement matrix keeps the frequencies but gives them no half-read vectors, CRLF input parses identically, and empty, blank, garbage and header-only inputs all yield zero frames without throwing.

- **A multi-frame `.xyz` opens as a trajectory, so ORCA's `_trj.xyz` is usable on its own** — ORCA writes every optimization as `<job>_trj.xyz`, one frame per cycle, with the energy in each comment line; the viewer read that file as a single structure and showed the first geometry. Frames are now read back to back (`count`, comment, atom rows, repeat) into a trajectory with the same frame stepper a log gets, and when **every** comment states an energy in a recognizable form (ORCA's `E -76.32109…`, xTB's `energy: …`, which share the layout) the convergence panel draws the energy curve for it — a partial series is refused rather than misaligned, since step k has to be frame k. A frame cut off by a killed job is dropped, not shown truncated, and a single-frame file takes the ordinary structure path untouched. Verified on `test/orca_test/h2o_trj.xyz` (5 frames, 5 energies, ending on the same geometry the `.out` parser reports for its final frame) and `h2o.xyz`, plus a trajectory truncated between frames and comments that carry no energy (**159 assertions** shared with the entry above).

### Changed

- **The README is one page with a language switch, instead of two files that could drift apart** — `README.md` now carries both languages: a title, then `## English` and `## 简体中文`, each opening with the same switcher (the current language in bold, the other one a link to `#english` / `#简体中文`). Choosing 简体中文 used to open a second page; now it jumps within the page, and one file cannot fall out of step with itself. The sections inside each language are demoted by one level so they nest under their language heading, and every existing in-page link keeps working because GitHub builds an anchor from the heading text, not from its level. `README.zh-CN.md` stays as a short pointer to the merged section, so links from outside (the Marketplace page, older commits, issues) still land somewhere. `check_readme_anchors.py` pins the result: it builds the anchor set GitHub would build — duplicate headings get a `-1`, which is exactly how a second heading silently steals a link — and checks that every in-page and cross-file anchor resolves (48 headings and 6 anchor links, all green, with both language anchors present in both directions).

- **An aromatic bond's dashed line now sits on the same side as its neighbours', so a ring reads as aromatic all the way round** — An aromatic bond is drawn as one solid line plus a six-dash line offset to one side, and that side used to fall out of the bond alone: the offset was the bond direction crossed with the world up axis, which for two aromatic bonds meeting at an atom points two different ways. Seen from the face of a ring, part of its bonds therefore carried the dashes inside and part outside — the ring never looked wholly aromatic, and a chain showed a band that zig-zagged from one side to the other. **The side is now a property of the structure, planned once per bond list.** Each connected aromatic system gets one plane (the cross products of the bond pairs meeting at its atoms, aligned on the least collinear pair and averaged, so one distorted bond cannot tilt a plane it is drawn in), and a walk over the system assigns every bond a side: at a **two-bond atom** the band passes straight through, which is what makes a ring or a chain show one continuous band; a **three-bond atom** is a branch, where each arm takes the side that points most nearly the way the arriving dash points, so a fused ring keeps the arm that stays in its own ring; and crossing a bond **no ring runs through** (a biaryl link) starts a new band, mirrored, because the arms on the far side are a ring of their own and must not inherit the side the band arrived on. A **bounded local repair** settles the atoms a fused system can still leave contradicted — a shared bond belongs to two rings and the walk only knows the one it came through — flipping only bonds whose flip leaves fewer contradicted atoms behind, so a ring that is already consistent is never touched. Finally the whole system is flipped if the majority of its bonds would point away from its own centre, which is what puts a ring's dashes **inside**, the way it is drawn on paper. The dash is now offset **in that plane** (crossed with the system normal) instead of against the world up axis, so it stays perpendicular to the bond and inside the plane however the structure is oriented. Nothing reads the atom order a file happens to use — a bond stored the other way round has its direction, and so its "left", flipped, and σ (which endpoint is atom1) accounts for it — and a crystal's wrapped bond is planned along the line it is actually drawn on, periodic shift included.

  **Nothing else about the drawing moved.** A double or triple bond's second and third lines keep the world-up offset they have always used, and every piece of a single, double or triple bond is placed by exactly the same arithmetic as before — pinned piece for piece against the old formula, with not a hair of difference. A dash piece keeps **no side and no plane of its own**: it reads both from the current plan at the moment it is placed, so a plan that is refreshed reaches every piece that is drawn afterwards without touching one of them, and a piece built before the refresh can never disagree with one built after it. The plan is therefore computed where the bond list is (re)built — a load, a frame or molecule switch, an edit, an Undo, an Import, an MM commit and a Move Atoms release — and again on the paths that turn or bend a geometry in place: a **fragment rotation drag** and an **Alt-drag rotation bake** re-plan as they turn (a ring's plane turns with it, so the dashes would otherwise tilt out of the ring for the length of the drag), a **manual normal-mode displacement** re-plans on every slider move, and a **Molecular Mechanics chunk** re-plans once per chunk. A pure translation changes no direction and does not re-plan, and the **normal-mode playback** deliberately keeps the equilibrium plan: it borrows the geometry for one cycle and puts it back, and a plan that changed sixty times a second could flip a side mid-animation. The plan is deterministic, writes no coordinate, and reads no colour or selection state. Verified against the **compiled** client — the real `createBond`/`placePiece` build the pieces, the real `poolPut` receives each cylinder's transform, and every assertion measures the offset that was actually drawn rather than re-deriving the rule — **2 871 assertions**: six hexagons and their reversed-bond twins, an arbitrarily rotated ring, a fused naphthalene, a zigzag chain, a collinear chain, a symmetric branch, a crystal bond across the cell boundary, an empty structure, a plan that is not there yet (every piece falls back to exactly the old single-sided offset), a ring turned 90° after its pieces were built (the dashes follow it into the new plane), the real rebuild path, the incremental Move Atoms release compared with a full rebuild piece for piece, and the source-level pins on which paths re-plan and which must not. The property that matters is checked on the drawings themselves: **no two-bond atom of any real structure puts its two dashes on opposite sides of the path**, across **15 structures from the project's own SMILES engine** (benzene, pyridine, pyrrole, imidazole, indole, azulene, naphthalene, anthracene, phenanthrene, pyrene, triphenylene, coronene, biphenyl, terphenyl, tryptophan) plus the synthetic corpus, and a per-ring measure — each bond against the centre of the smallest ring it belongs to — puts every dash of a single ring, of naphthalene, of indole, of azulene and of a biaryl inside its own ring. **Seven mutations** — the side always +1 again, the world-up offset back in place of the plane, the placement ignoring the plan altogether, the repair never flipping, a branch arm ignoring the band, the biaryl crossing not mirrored, the inside vote skipped — each fail the matching assertions, so none of the checks is vacuous. A polycyclic system cannot put every bond inside *both* of its rings (a bond has two sides), so a shared bond takes one of them: measured per ring, naphthalene and indole reach **100 %** and anthracene **94 %**. Cost, measured in the same Node sandbox as the entries above and against the very same rebuild with only this pass removed: **0.04 ms** on a benzene ring, **0.07 ms** on tryptophan, **4.1–4.4 ms** on a 1171-atom fused-ring structure (1 833 aromatic bonds) against a **64–67 ms** whole bond rebuild, and **37–66 ms** on an 8 429-atom graphene sheet (13 529 aromatic bonds) against a **0.6–1.0 s** one — a linear pass over the aromatic bonds, with the bridge search it needs taken only when some atom has three aromatic bonds. A structure with **no aromatic bond** pays a single scan and allocates nothing (the plan arrays are not even created), and **nothing here runs while the view is idle**: the pass is repeated only where a geometry is turned or relaxed — a fragment-rotation frame, an Alt-drag rotation frame, an MM chunk — which is 0.07 ms per frame on a drug-sized molecule and a few ms on a large fused system. The piece count and the placement arithmetic are untouched, so a structure draws exactly the cylinders it drew before.

### Fixed

- **An ORCA `.out` was only half read, and a Gaussian `.out` was not read at all** — Four faults, all of which came from deciding the format by extension and the charge by position. **The first frame carried no charge or multiplicity**: the parsed values were captured when the block was seen, but ORCA prints the `SCF SETTINGS` that state them *after* the first geometry, so the frame the file opens on — the one the info panel and the bond perception read — had none; they are now filled in on every frame that lacks them, while a frame that already has them keeps its own (a compound run can change the charge between jobs). **A Gaussian log saved as `.out` came back empty**: `.out` was routed to the ORCA parser unconditionally, so a Gaussian `.out` — a very common name for one — produced no structure at all; the format is now decided by **content**, with each detector matching a string only one program writes (Gaussian and ORCA are both routinely told to write `.out`, xTB shares `.log`), and the extension is not consulted at all. **An ORCA `.out` opened through Diff, Import or Merge came back empty** for the same reason in the single-frame path, which only ever asked the Gaussian parser; it now goes through the same dispatch, so it arrives with its charge and multiplicity. **A truncated geometry block became a short molecule**: a job killed mid-write left the rows that had been written, and they parsed into a structure with its last atoms missing, silently losing their bonds; a frame whose atom count disagrees with the count every other block in the file agrees on is now dropped (`test/orca_test/h2o.out` cut inside its final block loses that frame and keeps the four complete cycles). The same pass made `.inp` robust in the other direction: it is ORCA's input extension, but a Gaussian input named `.inp` — which the ORCA parser cannot read — now falls back to the GJF parser instead of opening empty. Verified with the Gaussian and xTB corpus in `test/` (**11 logs**, 9 Gaussian and 2 xTB, each still recognised and parsed with no empty frame and no exception), with the same bytes under four different names producing the same parser, with the Gaussian logs renamed to `.out` still taking the Gaussian path, and with a Gaussian `.inp` and an ORCA `.inp` both read correctly.

- **Ctrl+A selected the page's text instead of the structure** — The key handler does prevent the browser's default, but that only decides the outcome when the key reaches the page first: a host that performs select-all itself, or a build where the handler is bypassed, still painted the whole view blue — toolbar labels, panel numbers, the status line — while the atoms were selected as well or not at all. The page is now **structurally not selectable text**: `body{user-select:none}` (inherited by every panel, so one rule covers the toolbar, the cards, the canvas and the status bar) with `user-select:text` restored only for **form fields** — they keep their normal typing, caret and selection behaviour — and for the **load-error block**, so a parse failure can still be copied into a bug report. Ctrl+A now means Select All of the structure in every state, and a drag that starts on the canvas and ends over a panel no longer leaves a selection smeared across it. Because nothing outside a field is selectable any more, the copy path no longer defers to a page text selection — that guard is gone — and a field's own text options stay the field's. Verified on the **built page** rather than on the source: the stylesheet carries both the standard and the prefixed `user-select` for the body, the field rule and the error-block exemption (a selector that appears in two rules — the error block has one for its position — is checked across both, so the exemption cannot hide behind the other rule), and nothing in the client script sets a selection policy. Three mutations — the page selectable again, the fields losing their text selection, the error block losing its exemption — fail the matching assertions (1, 2 and 1), and the keyboard suite's copy case now pins that Ctrl+C copies the structure unconditionally. **48** selection assertions and **100** keyboard assertions pass.

## [1.0.2] - 2026-10-26

### Added

- **A saved file now says where it came from and what was done to it** — The viewer wrote a structure out with whatever title the file happened to carry, or with "Modified structure", so a saved geometry arrived detached from its history: the file it was taken from, the step of a trajectory it was cut at, and the edits that produced it (deleted atoms, a translation, an optimisation, a normal-mode displacement) were nowhere in it. Every format that owns a title or comment line now carries one line of provenance, built from what actually happened: `MV: from opt.log, frame 12 of 40, Step 12; 2 atoms deleted, translated, displaced along mode 7 (38.2 cm-1) +0.45 A`. It names the **source file**, the **frame** of an optimisation trajectory (with that step's own label when the log carries one) or the molecule of a VMD script, then the **edits in the order they were made** — atoms added/deleted/replaced, hydrogens added, translated, rotated, bond length/angle/dihedral adjusted, MM-optimized, disorder removed, files merged in, supercell bounds — and finally any **manual normal-mode displacement**, with each mode's number, its frequency (an imaginary one marked `i`) and its amplitude. Every clause is recorded at the operation that performs it instead of being inferred from the coordinates at save time, because "was this edited?" cannot be read off a structure; and the record travels in the **Undo** snapshot, so undoing an edit takes its clause off the title together with the geometry. **Restore initial** on a Molecular Mechanics run takes the optimisation clause back the same way.

  The line goes wherever the format can hold one — the XYZ comment, the Gaussian title card, the MOL2 molecule name, the MDL title line (which used to be blank), a PDB **TITLE** record wrapped at 60 characters with PDB's own continuation number, the MOPAC title, an ORCA comment line above the keywords, and the POSCAR/CUBE/VESTA headers — while formats with no title line (Turbomole `$coord`, CIF) are written exactly as before. The file's **own title** is appended after a `|` only when it describes the structure on screen: a trajectory's title belongs to the geometry the file opens on — a log's title card is that first structure's comment, and a frame switch does not rewrite it — so saving frame 79 of a 79-frame log must not end with frame 1's `Step 1 (Charge=0 Mult=1)`, which says the opposite of the frame clause beside it; the same holds for a molecule list, where a molecule after the first does not claim the script's title. A **GJF keeps its own title card**: the provenance line is written first and the original title lines follow it verbatim, so nothing the user wrote is lost, while lines an earlier save wrote are filtered out, so a file the viewer produced can be saved again without provenance stacking on provenance (the same `MV: ` marker makes the original-title tail drop itself when it is one of ours). The wording is deliberately short and **ASCII** (`A`, `cm-1`): a Gaussian title card is read by programs that are happier without non-ASCII, and a displaced structure's "Å" is no reason for one. The line is capped at **190 characters**, where whole clauses drop off the end behind a `...` rather than being cut mid-word, and a long original title is the part that gives way first.

  Implemented as one module: a state block (source file, merged files, edit list, committed displacements) declared beside the view state so no early path can read it undefined, the formatter beside `doSave`, and one `provMark(...)` call at each place that changes the geometry or the atom list — seventeen of them, from the four atom-edit dialogs to an Alt-drag, a Rotate Group commit, an MM run, a frame switch and the displacement slider. Verified by cutting the real code out of the compiled page and driving `doSave` against a stub host — **61 assertions**: the title each of XYZ / GJF / MOL2 / MDL / PDB / MOPAC / ORCA is actually written with, frame and step labelling, counted edits merging (three deletions read as one clause), the order of clauses, imaginary frequencies, a live versus a committed displacement (a committed one survives the session that made it), undo removing exactly the clause it undoes (and the snapshot being a deep copy, not a reference), an import naming its file once, a GJF keeping its own title lines with no duplication on a round trip, PDB wrapping, the 190-character cap and what it drops first, the supercell clause read from the bounds rather than marked, and a source-less structure falling back to the file it was merged from. The wiring is pinned by extracting each commit point's own source and looking for its mark — a formatter-only test would pass with the marks missing — **18 such checks**, the four atom-edit dialogs and the three geometry dialogs included.

- **Manual Displacement writes normal modes into the coordinates, as a combination** — The Modes panel could play a vibration but not hold it: the playback borrowed the geometry for one sine cycle and put it back, which left no way to inspect a distorted structure — its bond lengths, its angles, its shape — or to keep it. A mode now carries **two separate selections**, one per half of the feature. The **circle** at the end of its row picks that mode for the displacement slider; the **row itself (or its ▶)** plays the vibration. They are independent on purpose: playing a mode no longer re-aims the slider, and picking a mode no longer starts a playback — so a mode can be watched without disturbing what the slider will write, and a mode can be picked for displacement without an animation running over it. The block names the picked mode, its circle stays filled, and the row currently carrying a displacement is marked with the orange edge whether or not it is the picked one. The panel then carries a **Manual Displacement** block: with a mode picked, drag the slider and the structure is set to `equilibrium + Σ (scale_k × mode_k)` on every move. **A mode is added to the distortion, not swapped for the previous one** — the normal modes of one frequency job are an orthogonal basis of the 3N-6 displacements (orthogonal under the mass-weighted inner product the printed vectors already carry), so the sum is exactly a point in normal-coordinate space, and displacing along a second mode is the same arithmetic as a scan along a line in that space. Nothing accumulates invisibly: the block lists every component (`#2 +0.50 · #1 +0.30`), every contributing row carries the orange edge, dragging a component back to 0 takes that mode out, and Reset clears all of them. The largest displacement of the *combination* is measured on the result and shown next to the list, because it is not the sum of the parts — two modes can partially cancel. The slider itself is a **proportion of one mode, from −1.00 to +1.00**, not a distance: 1.00 moves that mode's most-moving atom by 1 Å, every other atom by its own share, so the same number means the same amount of the same mode on any structure, and each mode's relative per-atom weights and phases are kept exactly as the calculation produced them. The block prints what the picked mode's share comes to next to it (`+0.50 · max 0.50 Å`), so the number is never the only thing on offer. Each write starts from the equilibrium snapshot taken when the session opens — never from the previous frame — so dragging back and forth cannot accumulate drift, one slider value always means one geometry, and a repeated event (`input` + `change`, a key repeat) writes nothing at all; a value that arrives outside the range (a programmatic call, a hand-edited message) is clamped instead of written. **Save As** writes the displaced geometry in any format, and the dialog states the composition it is about to write instead of leaving the modes to be discovered in the file.
