  Three states are kept apart so a *pose* can never be mistaken for a *structure*. `vdBase` is the equilibrium the offsets are measured from; `vdComp` is the list of components actually in the structure, each with that mode's unit displacement; `vdTarget` is the mode the panel points at, whose share the slider edits — so picking or watching a mode writes nothing, and only a slider move adds a component or changes one ("Drag to add this mode to the distortion", "In the distortion: drag to change its share, 0 takes it out"). A session is a **pending rewrite of every atom**, which is why arbitration is the feature's other half: `pushUndo` — the door every coordinate edit opens with (bond length / angle / dihedral, add / delete / replace atom, batch delete, Add H, Import, Rotate Group, Move Atoms, Alt-drag, a Molecular Mechanics run) — closes the session first and keeps the geometry it produced, so the edit starts from the distorted structure and its own snapshot captures that structure; a frame switch, a molecule switch and an Undo drop the session instead, because the structure it was measured from is gone; a live Molecular Mechanics run refuses the slider and says so (the run owns those coordinates), and a Move Atoms session is committed before the slider takes over. **Reset** removes every component, writes the equilibrium back exactly and takes the session's undo snapshot with it, so a distortion that is undone leaves the stack as it found it; dragging the last component back to 0 does the same.

  The playback is the deliberate exception, and it is what makes the two usable one after the other. A manual displacement is a structure, a playback a pose: playing any mode swings from the session's **undisplaced** equilibrium — never from the distorted atoms, and never from the other components either — and stopping writes the whole combination back. So a mode always animates the motion the calculation produced rather than that motion on top of an arbitrary offset, watching modes never costs the distortion, and the user can drag, watch, come back and drag again. Without a session the playback is exactly what it was.

  Verified against the shipping code: the page is built the way the host builds it (the client script compiles, the style block balances, exactly one slider is in the document, and it carries `min="-1" max="1" step="0.01"`) and the functions are sliced out of that same built script and driven in a stub DOM — **107 assertions**: the panel's initial state (no mode picked, slider disabled, empty component list, a hint that names the circle), a row click playing a mode while leaving the slider unarmed, a circle click arming the slider without starting a playback and without disturbing the one already running, a click that starts on a circle not reaching the row's play handler, a first component at 0.5 (the most-moving atom 0.50 Å out), a second mode **added** rather than substituted (both components in the list, both rows marked, every atom carrying both displacements, still one undo snapshot), editing one component leaving the other alone, a component removed by dragging it back to 0 (the rest of the combination intact), the largest displacement of the combination measured on the result rather than summed, the two ends of the range (±1.00 → 1.00 Å, the readout following), values past the range clamped at both ends, one snapshot per session, a playback of another mode swinging around the pure equilibrium and restoring the whole combination when it stops, picking a mode writing nothing, Reset clearing every component and dropping the snapshot while keeping the picked mode, an edit committing the session (and the next session measuring from the committed geometry), a mode whose displacements do not match the structure disabling the slider, a write refused when the atom list changes under the session, a drag refused during a Molecular Mechanics run (and the thumb put back where the geometry is), a rebuilt panel restoring the component list, the row markers, the circle and the slider, and negative shares reading back with a minus sign and the right distance.

### Changed

- **Trajectory playback now ends on the last structure instead of looping, and runs faster** — Auto Play used to wrap from the last frame straight back to the first, so the one structure a run usually ends on — the geometry it converged to — could never be held: it was replaced by frame one on the very next tick, and a trajectory of a few hundred steps was a carousel with no end to watch. Playback now **stops on the last frame**: the last structure keeps its own dwell like every other one, then the timer clears itself, the button returns from ⏸ Stop to ⏵ Play, and the converged geometry stays on screen to be rotated, measured or saved. Pressing Play while that frame is showing means "run it again", so it restarts from the first frame rather than doing nothing at all; a single-frame structure still plays nothing, as before. The step interval drops from **500 ms to 350 ms**, which is the difference between reading a trajectory as a structure-by-structure walk and watching a slideshow — each step is a still that has to be legible on its own, so the gain is deliberately modest, and it now lives in one named constant (`FRAME_PLAY_MS`) instead of a literal inside the timer call so the two places that reason about the cadence agree. Nothing else moved: the guards are untouched, so a frame or molecule switch, an Undo, an Import, a normal-mode playback, a Molecular Mechanics run and a Move Atoms or Edit Coordinates session all still end an auto-play, and starting one still ends those first. Verified against the real compiled client, cut out of the page and driven in a stub DOM on a fake clock — **44 assertions**: the walk from frame 1 to the last frame with no jump back, the stop itself (timer cleared, button text and class restored, further ticks writing nothing), the restart-from-first on a second Play, manual Prev/Next still clamped at both ends, a one-frame structure, and the other writers still being ended first. The same assertions run against the pre-change logic fail **21** of them, including the wrap to frame 1 and the 500 ms interval, so none of them passes on the old behaviour; the whole page's client script is also re-parsed after the edit (7532 lines) to catch a template-level syntax slip.

- **The toolbar is two rows, so the buttons at its end no longer sit behind a scroll gesture** — The toolbar was one row, and with the editing modes, the structure actions and the trajectory/molecule navigation all in it, the shipped button set needed about **1700 px**. On any ordinary panel width that meant everything from **Select Atoms** on — Select Atoms, Box Select, Diff, Undo, Save As, Reset View and both navigations — was past the right edge and could only be reached by dragging the bar sideways, so the functions one uses constantly (Save As, Reset View, the trajectory stepper) sat behind a gesture while the modes one picks once a session filled the visible half. It is now **two rows**: the modes and the structure-editing tools stay on the first (View, Bond Length, Bond Angle, Dihedral, Rotate Group, Move Atoms, MM Optimize, Add/Delete/Replace Atom, Add H, Import Structure), and the selection pair with the structure and view actions and the navigation goes on the second (Select Atoms, Box Select, Diff, Undo, Save As, Reset View, the ◀ n/N ▶ frame stepper, the molecule selector). **Each row scrolls on its own**, and only when its own content does not fit, so a narrow panel still reaches every button and one row scrolling cannot drag the other along. The buttons themselves are untouched — same elements, same ids, same click handlers, same mode highlighting, all of which resolve by id or by `[data-mode]` across the document — so this is layout only; nothing about switching modes, frames or molecules, about playback, or about any dialog changed.

  The row height is what makes the scrollbar safe. A row's scrollbar lives *inside* the row, and `scrollbar-width:thin` is about **10 px**, so a 30 px row lost its buttons to the bar the moment it overflowed (the first cut of this change did exactly that, and the layout harness caught it: a 21 px button in a 20 px content box). Rows are **34 px**, which leaves the content box taller than the tallest button whether the bar is showing or not, and the height does not depend on what a row happens to contain — it stays 34 px with a trajectory loaded, with a Beta structure's extra button, with a molecule list, and with nothing but four actions, so the second row does not jump as files are opened. The toolbar's fixed 36 px height is gone in favour of two stacked rows; the canvas is `flex:1` and simply takes the remainder (verified: toolbar 73 px, status bar 24 px, canvas exactly the rest of the window at every size). Verified against the real `<style>` and the real markup in a real engine (headless Edge) at four window sizes — **192 assertions**: both rows exist and hold exactly the buttons they should, in order (row 1's thirteen items including its separator; row 2's seven (the selection pair, a separator, the four actions) plus the frame and molecule navs, whose five and three children are intact), no item clipped vertically out of its row, each row `overflow-x:auto` / `overflow-y:hidden`, the content box fitting its tallest item so a scrollbar can never eat a button, the scrollbar appearing **only** when that row overflows (height difference 0 while everything fits, 10 px when the rows are squeezed to 300 px, in which state each row still scrolls on its own with `scrollLeft` moving and clips nothing), the two rows staying the same height across all three content states, and the canvas taking exactly the rest of the window below both rows. The same markup was also laid out the old way as a control: at a 970 px viewport the single bar is 1700 px wide and leaves Select Atoms, Box Select, Diff, Undo, Save As, Reset View and both navs off-screen, while the second row needs 741 px and fits entirely, with the first now 973 px — under a 990 px viewport nothing needs a drag at all — which is the measurement this change exists for.

## [1.0.1] - 2026-10-19

### Added

- **Alt-drag writes the drag into the coordinates, so the orientation you set is the geometry you save** — A View-mode drag only ever moved the picture: `rotQuat` and the pan offset changed while the atoms stayed exactly where the file put them, which left no way to orient a structure for a figure — or to bring several structures into one frame — without an external editor. Holding **Alt** while pressing now turns the same drag into an edit: **left** rotates the structure and **right/middle** translates it, the drag moves the atom coordinates, and **Save As** writes them in any format. The picture is identical to a plain drag, and that is the design: the rotation is written about the structure's own centre (the centroid, which a rotation about it leaves fixed) and the translation along the same screen direction the pan uses, each converted into the structure's own frame, so a baked rotation composed with the view is exactly the view rotation it replaced — no double turn and no jump on release. The translation freezes the pan offset for the drag and moves the atoms by `R⁻¹·Δpan`; on release the pan the drag would have reached is committed as well, and because the centre follows the atoms by exactly that translation the two cancel, so the picture stays where the drag put it while the file carries the movement. **Reset View** afterwards returns the camera to the file's axes: the orientation now lives in the coordinates. One undo snapshot per drag, dropped again for an Alt-click that moves nothing; no bond is re-derived, because a rigid transform changes no distance; the rotation centre and the surface patches are refreshed once at the release rather than per frame.

  Arbitration is the feature's other half. Only the press decides — releasing Alt mid-drag keeps the drag an edit, and a drag not started with Alt never becomes one halfway through, so no gesture changes meaning under the cursor. A running **vibration** and a **frame auto-play** are ended before the snapshot is taken (both write the same coordinates), and the drag is also ended by `stopMoveSession`, the door every structure-replacing entry already goes through (a mode switch, a frame, a molecule, an Undo, an Import), so a structure that was replaced can never be written from a stale snapshot; `bakeWriteAtoms` additionally refuses to write if the atom array changed length under the drag. Arrow keys are ignored while the drag runs (a view turn would apply the rotation a second time), **Auto Rotate is not launched from a bake drag** (the drag was an edit, not a throw) while the new press still stops any spin the way every left press does. Where the coordinates are tied to something the drag cannot move with them — **Diff** mode, which has two structures and two view states, and **crystal** structures, whose coordinates belong to a cell — the drag stays view-only, the status bar says why once, and the View-mode card's own line states it. That line lives in an **Edit Coordinates** card directly above the **Auto Rotate** card, so the feature is discoverable exactly where the mode it belongs to is switched on.

  Verified by booting the real client script in a stub sandbox (real three.js math, the real mouse listeners, a 2175-atom / 3309-bond structure) and driving the actual drags — **49 assertions**. The core one compares two boots: the same mouse motion as a plain drag and as an Alt-drag, with every atom's world position required to agree to 1e-12 Å (measured 4.4e-14 for the rotation, 4.1e-14 for the translation), and it fails at 0.36 Å when the frame conjugation is removed, so the check is not vacuous. A rotation started from an already-turned view is compared the same way (7.3e-14), and every interatomic distance is shown unchanged (worst 5.3e-15 Å). A baked translation moves every atom by one screen vector with no world-z drift (1.8e-15) and leaves the pan frozen until the release commits it. **Save** is driven through its real modal and the emitted XYZ parsed back: 2175 atoms, every coordinate within 5e-7 Å of the baked ones. Undo restores the pre-drag geometry bit for bit; an Alt-click without movement adds no undo step; another mode, a crystal and a comparison never bake and say why; Auto Rotate's speed stays 0 after a bake drag; an empty structure does nothing. Cost on that structure is **4.3 ms per mousemove** (atoms, all bond pieces, snapshot replay) — well inside a frame.

- **Auto Rotate — flick the molecule, or hold an arrow key, and it keeps turning** — A new **Auto Rotate** toggle appears in a small **View**-mode panel at the bottom-right of the canvas, so it is on screen exactly while the View mode is the active one, it keeps clear of the bottom-left corner the orientation axes gizmo occupies, and the toolbar does not grow another button. **Two inputs feed one angular velocity.** A **left-drag that rotates the view** (or a one-finger touch drag) arms a spin: on release the molecule is handed the angular velocity the drag was *released with*, so it keeps turning about a fixed world axis, in the direction the drag was turning it. An **arrow key, held**, does the same job as an accelerator: it spins the view up rather than turning it at a fixed rate, **the longer the hold the faster** (0.5 rad/s plus 2.5 rad/s for every second held, up to the same cap the flick uses), the axis is the one the pressed keys name — Right/Left on Y and Down/Up on X, the very same mapping a drag uses — and **releasing the key hands over the speed it reached**, which then becomes the free rotation. Either way the molecule keeps turning until something stops it. Distance over time is the release speed, so dragging farther or flicking faster spins faster; the drag speed is measured over **only the last 120 ms**, so a drag brought to a standstill before the button comes up releases at a standstill instead of throwing whatever speed it reached earlier. The speed is capped so a wild flick cannot blur the view, and a release below a small floor is read as a stop rather than a near-invisible drift; the toggle turns the feature off and clears any spin in flight, and its own label reads **On**/**Off** with that state. **A right or middle press pans and leaves the spin running** — the one asymmetry the interaction asks for — while a left press, **Reset View**, leaving Diff mode, **leaving View mode** and switching the toggle off each end it, and the **first** arrow key of a hold takes over from any spin already running (keys pressed while another is held, and a held key's auto-repeat, deliberately do not, or every repeat would restart the ramp from its base and the hold could never speed up). So the free spin can never run underneath a manual rotation and compose with it into a drift, and it can never outlive the panel that is its only switch. Ctrl+arrow in Move Atoms rotates the selected fragment and never reaches any of this. The spin is **view-only**: it writes the same rotation quaternion a drag writes (`rotQuat`, or the grabbed side's `diffRotQuat` in Diff mode) and never touches the coordinates, so no edit, playback, optimisation run or Move Atoms session can be disturbed by it, and it works on the two halves of a comparison independently. Verified by running the real client functions out of the compiled page against the real three.js quaternion math — **71 assertions**, all green: the release speed equals the drag's own speed and doubles with the distance; the axis follows the drag's direction on all four axis-aligned gestures; the key ramp lands exactly on `base + rate × held`, is strictly faster for a longer hold, caps at the same ceiling, maps each of the four keys to the drag's own axis, gives two keys a diagonal unit axis, and hands its reached speed to the free rotation on release; a drag held still before release, and one below the floor, produce no spin; a wild flick is clamped while the axis stays unit; the panel is anchored bottom-right and registered that way with the layout solver, shown for View and hidden for every other mode, and the toolbar no longer carries the button; the toggle, its label, Reset View, leaving Diff and leaving View all end it, and a right press does not; the frame integration reproduces the drag's own composed rotation to under 1e-6 rad and advances the angle at exactly the launched rate; and a five-second background gap is clamped instead of jumping. Removing the panel's position rule, the bottom-right registration, the label update, the ramp, the keyup session reset, the first-key guard and the drive call sites one at a time makes exactly the matching assertions fail, so none of them is vacuous.

### Fixed

- **The View-mode cards and the Show Results button no longer sit under each other** — The View-mode controls had grown to carry both Auto Rotate and the Edit Coordinates description in one panel, and neither that panel's two halves nor the Diff panel's **Show Results** button took part in the panel layout as boxes of their own, so the bottom-right corner could end up drawn on top of itself: the reopened comparison button under the View controls, and one half of those controls under the other once the panel was tall. **Auto Rotate** and **Edit Coordinates** are now two cards, each registered with its own collision box in `layoutPanels()` (bottom-right, in that reading order, both shown exactly while View mode is the active one), and the egg list and the **Show Results** button are registered too, so each of them is given way to instead of covered. The Edit Coordinates card carries the same line the combined panel showed. Verified by booting the real client script against a 1000x700 absolute-positioning model and running the real `layoutPanels()` over six combinations of visible boxes — **15 assertions**: the two cards alone (order and bottom-right anchor included), the Diff results panel open, the reopen button alone, the egg list with the supercell panel, everything that fits at once, and a mode round trip that hides and restores both cards; no pair of boxes overlaps in any of them, and the card still explains the feature afterwards.

- **Scalar (Beta) colouring now covers the bonds, not only the spheres** — Opening a force-error PDB — or any structure whose PDB columns 61-66 carry a per-atom scalar — painted every **sphere** by the scalar but left every **bond** in its element colours, so the force-error picture came out as two different colourings at once: a carbon–oxygen bond stayed grey-and-red across two spheres that were both sitting on the scalar gradient, and it was the bonds, not the spheres, that the eye followed across the surface. Each **bond half now reads the very colour its atom is drawn with** — the half nearer an atom takes that atom's scalar colour while colouring is on, and its element colour otherwise, or whenever that atom carries no scalar, so a partially-scalared structure keeps its unscalared atoms *and* their bonds consistent. Nothing new is stored: the colour comes from the same view-state function that paints the atoms, so the two can only ever agree, and a bond is re-coloured from the current mode by every path that already rebuilds it — the toggle, an Undo, an Import, a frame or molecule switch, the left side of a comparison, a Move Atoms release and an MM commit — with no backup or restore to keep in step. The bonds draw from an instance colour buffer, so following the scalar costs no extra geometry, material or draw call however many distinct scalars the structure has. The definition-order hazard the scalar mode already documents is honoured on the new path as well: the initial scene build runs **before** the colour constants are assigned, where the colour function returns nothing and the bonds fall back to element colours, and only the auto-colour and the toggle (both after the constants) repaint them. Verified on the real client script — bond pieces are built by the shipping `createBond` under both modes and every half is compared against its own atom's drawn colour: **8 assertions**, all green, and every Beta assertion fails when the change is reverted, so they are not vacuous.

- **A large structure no longer takes the window's WebGL down with it** — Opening a big structure used to blank the view, and from then on **every** file opened in that window failed with `Error creating WebGL context.` — including files that had drawn fine a minute earlier. The structure in question has 2175 atoms, and the viewer built **16081 separate three.js objects** for it: one sphere per atom and, for the 3309 bonds, one cylinder per half plus six more for every aromatic bond's dashed line (13906 cylinders in all, nearly 8000 of them from aromatic bonds alone). Every one of those objects had its own geometry **and its own material**, so the first frame also had to create roughly 64 000 GL buffers (three attributes and an index per geometry). A per-object material is the expensive half: three.js re-uploads a material's whole uniform set before any draw call whose material id has changed, so ~16 000 distinct materials meant on the order of a million uniform writes in a single frame — enough driver work in one frame to reset the GPU process on Windows, and that process is shared by every webview in the window. Once it is gone no page can create a context, which is exactly why the failure followed every later file and could only be cleared by reloading the window; three.js's message names the symptom and not the cause, and the page had no handler for a context lost mid-session, so the first structure simply went blank.

  Three changes remove it. **Bond cylinders are no longer meshes.** A bond piece is now a plain record — which bond, which half, its radius and colour, the aromatic dash window, the multiple-bond offset — and one shared unit cylinder draws all of them through a single `InstancedMesh`: the piece's radius and length live in the instance matrix and its colour in the instance colour buffer, so the bonds of a structure of any size cost **one draw call and one geometry** instead of one object, one geometry and one material per cylinder. **Atom geometry and materials are pooled**: a sphere is shared by every atom of the same radius and a material by every atom with the same colour, occupancy and highlight state — the highlight (selected, diff-highlighted, plain) is now *chosen* from that pool instead of being written into a per-atom material, which is what made one material per atom necessary before. **Placement is one routine.** A piece's position, orientation and length are read off the two atoms it spans by the same function that a scene build, a Move Atoms drag, a streamed MM chunk and a normal-mode playback all call, so building a piece and animating it can no longer drift apart (the old code kept two copies of that arithmetic — one in the builder, one in the playback — which is also why an aromatic bond's dashes stretched correctly but a multiple-bond line's offset plane could disagree with itself). Measured on the 2175-atom structure: **16 081 objects → 2 177, 16 081 geometries → 6, 16 081 materials → 7** (and ~64 000 GL buffers → ~26), with all 13 906 bond pieces re-placed in **13.6–15.5 ms** and the whole scene rebuilt in **34–40 ms** (min–median of five runs in a Node sandbox, where property access is slower than in the page). The comparison (Diff) scene goes through the same machinery, with two pools so a highlighted bond keeps the orange glow its material carries, and its two sides no longer build a mesh and a material per atom and per bond either.

  The failure path itself is hardened too. A context lost mid-session is caught (`preventDefault`, a message on screen, and a scene rebuild when — and if — it comes back) instead of leaving a blank canvas; the context is released when the page is really going away rather than held until the panel is disposed; and a renderer that cannot be created at all now names what actually fixes it — *"the GPU process of the editor window is unavailable. Run "Developer: Reload Window" (or restart the editor)"* — instead of only repeating three.js. The material pool is a cache that outlives the meshes drawing with it (so an edit or a colour toggle does not re-create every material) and is dropped once it passes a bound no real structure reaches; the atom half of it is the only part that can grow with the data, because **scalar colouring gives every distinct scalar its own colour** — measured at 524 entries for a 2175-atom structure carrying a per-atom scalar, still an order of magnitude below one material per drawn object, and the bonds stay a single instanced mesh either way. One visible detail changed: a dashed aromatic piece is now cut from the shared 8-segment cylinder rather than owning a 6-segment one, on a cylinder 0.08 Å in radius, where the difference is below a pixel.

  Verified two ways, and the second way is the one that matters. The client is booted in a stub DOM and driven directly, and it is *also* booted from the page the **real extension host** builds (`openCustomDocument` + `getHtmlForWebview` run against a stubbed `vscode` module, so no payload field can be missing the way a hand-written one can — VMD payloads, crystal expansion, 647-frame logs, optimisation steps and atom groups all arrive the way they do in the editor). For a 15-file corpus plus the three VMD script samples, a crystal CIF and a `.tcl` that sets a 0.5 sphere scale, the number of drawn cylinders is identical to the pre-change builder's and **every cylinder's position, direction, length, radius and colour matches** to within one float32 ulp (worst delta 9.5e-7 Å, which is the instance buffer's own precision); a control reference that draws every cylinder 2e-3 Å longer is detected in every file, so the comparison is not vacuous. The **draw pass itself is simulated** — one call per mesh, `object.count` instances per instanced mesh, invisible objects and materials skipped — because asserting on the instance buffers is exactly how "the buffer is full but the mesh draws nothing" passes a green run. The features that move bonds were driven through that same client: a drag re-places exactly the pieces of the bonds touching the moved atoms and nothing else, and the release path — re-derivation included — equals a full re-derivation piece for piece; a whole-structure playback equals a full rebuild; a rotation-centre change carries every piece with the geometry; a supercell grown and shrunk back re-places and re-draws everything; selection and diff highlights come from the shared pool and clear back to the shared plain material, on a partial-occupancy atom too; leaving a comparison restores the single structure's drawing. Edge cases: a bond the script refuses to draw, two atoms dragged onto each other (no NaN in any instance matrix), an empty structure, scalar colouring on and off, a crystal's 84 periodic-image bonds (1422 pieces placed outside the asymmetric unit), a lost context (reported, then rebuilt on restore), a page-hide release, and a renderer that cannot be created at all (the message names the fix). **351 assertions, 0 failures** — and the harness found three defects on the way, *after* an earlier 208-assertion run had already gone green: the pool's early-out did not re-attach its mesh to the group a rebuild had just cleared (every bond would have vanished on the first edit), the refilled mesh left `count` at 0 so it drew **no** instances (the bonds were invisible), and entering a comparison stopped applying the left-hand highlight because the flags it sets are only materialised by a later recompute. Each was fixed and the assertions that catch it were kept.

## [1.0.0] - 2026-10-10

### Added

- **MM Optimize relaxes the selected atoms with a built-in force field, and every other atom stays exactly where it was** — A new **MM Optimize** toolbar button (immediately right of Move Atoms) opens a panel that carries the force field's own parameter list, the settings a run may use, the atoms it would move and the button that starts it. **Every settable parameter is a selection control** — the term switches (non-bonded, torsion, improper), the optimiser's settings (convergence max|F|, step limit, time budget, step size), the non-bonded cutoff, and a precision preset that fills all of them in one click — and each control opens on **the module's own default**, because the choices, their labels and their defaults are generated from the same constants the optimiser runs on; the module validates every value again (type, range, clamp), so a panel can offer choices without being able to put a run into an unreasonable state. Selection is the viewer's usual one: a click in the mode, or a selection carried in from Select Atoms / Box Select / Replace Atom / Add H / Rotate Group / Move Atoms (it survives the switch in both directions), and the panel's list and button state follow it. Pressing **Start Optimization** sends the structure, the selection and the chosen settings to the extension host, where the force field runs as a session the host steps and reports (see below), and the geometry it reaches is applied as **one Undo step**; the panel then reports energy before → after, the number of steps and why it stopped (converged / energy converged / step or time budget), the final max and RMS force, the largest displacement, and how many of each term were live. The status bar carries the same summary, so the effect of a run is readable without opening anything. Guards: a periodic structure and a two-structure comparison refuse the mode (the force field has no image atoms and no way to choose a side of the comparison), a click without a selection says so instead of guessing, a second click while a request is in flight is ignored, and an answer that arrives after the view changed (diff opened, crystal loaded, atoms added or removed) is refused rather than written into the wrong structure. The atoms that move are exactly the atoms the request named — the answer carries new coordinates for them and nothing else — and the refresh afterwards is the Move Atoms release path, so the bonds of the moved region are re-judged by the same perception module a fresh load would use, the unaffected bond meshes are kept, and the rotation centre follows the new geometry. Frame stepping, molecule switching and undo clear the panel's last-run block, which would otherwise describe a geometry that is no longer on screen.

  **A run is steerable while it works.** The extension host steps the session in chunks and reports each one, so the panel *watches* the optimisation instead of waiting for it: every chunk's coordinates are written straight into the structure (spheres and only the bonds that touch the moved atoms — the Move Atoms drag path, not a scene rebuild), the energy and the max/RMS force are drawn on two canvases against the step, and a line under them names the step, the energy and its change. **Pause** stops the pump and keeps the session, so **Resume** continues from exactly that geometry, step size, conjugate direction and pair list — a paused-and-resumed run was verified to equal the never-paused one, bit for bit — and the wall-clock budget excludes the time spent paused (a run whose 1 ms budget would have expired during a 200 ms pause still took its steps after the resume). **Restore initial** writes the geometry the run started from back, as one Undo step. **Leaving the mode interrupts the run**: another tool, a frame, a molecule, an Undo, an Import, a comparison — and disposing the panel — each cancel the session, so a late chunk can never keep writing coordinates into a structure the user has moved on from, and every message carries the run's id, so an answer from a replaced run is dropped rather than applied. Closing the panel with its × only hides it: a small **MM Optimize** button beside the Convergence / Modes / Route buttons reopens it, and its label doubles as the run's state ("running – step N" / "paused") while a run works with the panel closed.

  **Two more controls, both module-owned.** A **Precision preset** fills every other control in one click: *Low* — a quick pre-optimisation (no non-bonded and no torsion terms, 0.4 kcal/mol/Å target, 120 steps, 1.5 s budget, 0.30 Å steps, 6 Å cutoff), *Medium* — exactly the module's defaults, the values every earlier release ran on — and *High* — a careful run (every term, 0.01 kcal/mol/Å, 2000 steps, 20 s, 0.05 Å, 10 Å). The values live in the module's own table and the panel only shows them, so a preset cannot drift from what runs; hand-editing any value turns the choice into *Custom* (the preset select is updated in place, so the panel is not rebuilt under the user's cursor), and a request always carries the explicit value of every control, so the preset is a base, never an override. Measured on the distorted ethane: medium converges in 51 steps to max|F| 0.027 kcal/mol/Å, high in 81 steps to 0.0093, low in 29 steps to 0.377 — `{preset:'medium'}` reproduces the no-options answer exactly (byte-identical), an unknown preset name is ignored, and `{preset:'high', maxIterations:50}` obeys the explicit 50-step cap. The **non-bonded cutoff** select (5–12 Å, default 8) moves the taper, the pair-list buffer and the search grid cell together; the default reproduces the previous behaviour bit for bit, the value is clamped (500 → 14 Å, −3 → 4 Å), no interaction survives past the cutoff (two methanes 0.5 Å beyond it give exactly the energy of two far apart, to the last bit of the summation order), and the analytic gradient inside every taper window (5, 6, 8, 10, 12 Å) still matches central finite differences — worst relative error 2.3e-7.

  **This is a restrained optimisation, not an isolated one**: a fixed atom is a full participant in every term it shares with a mobile atom — its bonds, angles, torsions, impropers and non-bonded contacts all pull on the selection — and only a term whose atoms are *all* fixed is dropped (it cannot change and would only add a constant). That includes the case a terminal group hangs off a frozen backbone: a dihedral's energy depends on all four of its atoms, so its term is live as soon as *any* of them is mobile, including a mobile flanking atom on a fixed central pair. The contract `evaluateForceField` hands out matches: `gradient[i]` is exactly zero for a fixed atom and non-zero for a mobile one that shares a term with it.

  The force field (`src/tools/mmOptimizer.ts`, one module, zero dependencies) takes its reference geometry from the plugin's own experience tables — `empiricalBondLength`, `empiricalBondAngle`, `empiricalVdwRadius`, the same windows Add H reads and the same numbers the SMILES → 3D engine projects its generated structures onto — so a relaxed bond is the bond generation would have produced. **Bond stretch** is harmonic with `k = 664.12·Z₁Z₂/r₀³` over effective charges and the table's `r₀` for the bond's order (aromatic bonds go through the table's aromatic entry, so benzene relaxes to 1.386 Å). **Angle bending** is harmonic towards the centre element's own theoretical angle (C 109.47° sp³ / 120° sp² / 180° sp, N 106.5°, O 104.5°, S 98°), read off the bond orders the centre already has the way the engine reads them; the window in which the term exerts force is **asymmetric** — a *compressed* angle beyond 30° of its target carries none, so a strained ring (cyclopropane's 60° internal angle) is left as drawn, while a *widened* one keeps pulling up to 60°, because an angle that is too open is a drawing to be corrected rather than a clash to be left alone. **Torsion** is three-fold staggered on an sp³–sp³ bond, two-fold planar where an sp² centre or a double/aromatic bond is involved — where a double bond's two-fold term makes cis and trans *both* minima, so it can straighten a group but never flip it inside out — and no term at all where hydrogen defines the four-atom set, which leaves a methyl group's rotation free and restrains only a chain's backbone and a planar group's conjugation. **Out-of-plane** restraints hold a three-coordinate sp² centre planar using the signed volume of its three bond vectors, scaled by a fixed reference area so the energy of a given out-of-plane height does not depend on the centre's own bond lengths. **Non-bonded** is Lennard-Jones with σ from the van der Waals radii and ε = 0.044–0.21 kcal/mol, excluding bonded pairs, a 1-4 pair at half strength, no charges, and switched off smoothly between 6 and 8 Å — a bare cutoff leaves a step of the pair's energy at the boundary (≈6e-4 kcal/mol), and a pair crossing it makes the energy discontinuous, which a line search cannot step through. Below 0.2 σ the potential is continued by its own tangent, so a pile-up of atoms stays finite (no Infinity, no NaN) while still being pushed apart. A **1-3 pair that comes inside 0.45 of its van der Waals sum is re-admitted as pure r⁻¹² repulsion**: an angle squeezed that far sits in the angle term's force-free window, and without this guard two neighbours can walk onto each other (see the verification below — that collapse was found and fixed by test, not by inspection).

  **Only the terms a mobile atom takes part in are evaluated** and the optimiser is a preconditioned Polak-Ribière conjugate gradient whose every accepted step must lower the energy, so a run is monotone by construction and the geometry it returns is never worse than the one it was given, converged or not. Two properties make the search able to walk down at all, both established by measurement: the step cap is a **trust radius on the whole direction** (the preconditioned vector is scaled so its largest atom step is the cap) rather than a per-atom clip — a clip decouples the trial from the line-search factor, so every attempt moves the clipped atom by the same distance and a step whose curvature cost exceeds its gradient gain can never be shrunk; and the **pair list is rebuilt only after an accepted step**, once an atom has drifted 0.5 Å, and the energy the next search compares against is re-evaluated from that list — rebuilding from a trial geometry would leave the search comparing energies from two different lists, which looks like a step-independent jump (the clash guard's membership depends on distance, and its energy is large). The whole call is bounded by atom count (3000), step count (500) and wall-clock time (5 s), and the geometry returned when a budget runs out is the best one found.

  Verified with the real module and the real client code driven against stubs. **Analytical gradients were checked against central finite differences** on water, ethane, butane, benzene and formaldehyde — 178 assertions, worst relative error **1.3e-8** — for the fully mobile case and for a half-mobile one, where the fixed atoms carry **exactly zero** gradient; the energy is translation-invariant to **1.2e-13 kcal/mol**. **Restrained, not isolated**: with a carbon fixed and only a hydrogen mobile, the hydrogen lands at the C–H table length **1.089992 Å** from the fixed atom (it has no other partner, so a run that ignored the fixed atom could not move it at all) and the same answer follows the fixed atom when it is moved; with three hydrogens of methane frozen and the fourth started far outside the angle window, it converges to **C–H 1.0901 Å and all three H–C–H angles exactly 109.47°**, staying 1.780 Å clear of the nearest frozen hydrogen (**before the clash guard it collapsed onto one, at an angle of 53°** — the finding that motivated the guard); a free hydrogen 1.46 Å from a frozen methane is pushed out to 2.276 Å by van der Waals alone; and a pentane whose terminal carbon is the only mobile atom feels the torsion of the frozen C2–C3 bond (98.117 vs 97.705 kcal/mol with the term switched off) and lands on a different geometry. Relaxation quality: ethanol, methane and benzene distorted by up to 0.25 Å come back with **every bond at its table length** (worst deviation 0.0015 Å — methane 0.0000, ethanol 0.0001, benzene 0.0014) and energies 52.66 → −0.24, 7.82 → 0.0000 and 165.74 → −3.69 kcal/mol; water lands on **0.96002 / 0.95998 Å and 104.495°**; a second run on the relaxed geometry moves nothing. Partial optimisation: for selections of a bonded atom plus its neighbours in PEG600 (73 atoms), 604426met (516), 1500752_2met (356) and test.pdb (216), **every unselected atom comes back bit-identical** and the runs take **2–21 ms**. The 19-file corpus: **19/19 improve**, every run monotone, the slowest 3.1 s inside its budget. Settings: the defaults the panel shows equal the defaults the module applies; a tighter tolerance never stops earlier; the step limit caps a run; each switch removes exactly its own terms (counts and energies); one step never travels further than the step cap, and a gentler cap still reaches the same minimum; clamps and nonsense payloads (negative, wrong-typed, absurd, non-object options) all fall back without throwing. The clash guard was checked not to fire anywhere it should not: **0 of the 12 engine-generated structures** trigger it, and on the corpus the 19 pairs it catches all sit in the two files whose coordinates are known to be damaged (`732742_0.gjf`, `ethane_distorted.gjf`). Robustness: empty arrays, a single atom, a missing element, NaN coordinates, coincident atoms, out-of-range and fractional indices, a self-bond, negative and NaN orders, unknown elements, duplicate selection, no bonds at all, malformed payloads and nonsense options all return a structured result **without throwing**, with finite coordinates and bounded time; 200 coincident atoms separate (6.0e12 → 3.0e9 kcal/mol in 253 steps); an over-cap selection is refused with the limit named; the answer is deterministic (two runs give identical coordinates). Two properties of the pair list are checked structurally because they are what make the energy reproducible: each pair is added by exactly one of its two mobile ends, and cells are keyed by their integer coordinates rather than by a hash (colliding keys would make one bucket be scanned twice, and an atom crossing a cell boundary would change the answer). The extracted client script parses, carries no backticks, and is asserted to declare its state and helpers before the code that uses them, to wire the mode into every list it belongs to (click-to-select, selection preservation, the crystal/diff guard, the status line, the panel layout), and to sit immediately right of Move Atoms; a stub-driven run of that script (**70 assertions**) shows the panel opening with its description and one select per settable parameter with its default preselected, a change on a control travelling with the next request, the close click hiding the panel and the same-mode toolbar button reopening it, the selection enabling and listing atoms, the request carrying a copy of the structure, the selection and the settings, the answer moving exactly the named atoms, one undo snapshot, the result block and status line, and refusals for a stale, out-of-range, NaN or failed answer — plus a crystal structure refusing the mode with the reason on the status bar (this round's work added **322 module assertions** — baseline equality, chunking, presets, cutoff finite differences at 5–12 Å, pause/resume and abandon — **81 client assertions** (the static wiring plus a stub-driven session: start → live coordinates → pause → resume → result → restore, one undo snapshot at the first write, a stale run id that writes nothing, and the navigation cancel) and **19 host assertions** driven through the compiled extension code, in which a whole streamed run — mmProgress → mmResult — is byte-identical to the module's single call, Pause really stops the pump, Resume continues it, and Cancel or a disposing panel stops it for good).

  **The optimiser is now a session, and the single call is the same code.** `beginOptimization` validates, builds the field and takes the first energy/force reading; `session.step(budget)` runs at most that many passes and reports where the run stands (energy, forces, steps and the coordinates of the mobile atoms); `session.finish()` gives the complete answer at any time — after convergence, after a budget ran out, or halfway through, which is exactly what a pause reports; `pause()` / `resume()` / `abandon()` steer it. `optimizeSelection` is begin → step(∞) → finish, so a caller that only wants an answer still gets one and the two paths cannot drift: after the refactor every field of every answer (coordinates included) is **byte-identical** to a baseline dumped from the build before it — 16 structure and option cases, from a single atom to a 20-atom pentane, including every term switch, a tighter tolerance, a gentler step cap and a step-budget cut-off — and identical again for chunk sizes of 1 / 3 / 7 / 1000 steps and for a pause/resume in the middle of each of those runs. The history a session keeps (one point per accepted step, step 0 = the input geometry, thinned past 4000 points) is the live curve's only source. Measured: a 20-atom pentane run of 500 steps streamed in 51 chunks costs about 28 ms against 28 ms for the single call once the code is warm (71 ms against 47 ms on the first, cold run — the chunk boundary itself is not measurable), with an identical answer, and its 501 history points were monotone within every pair-list epoch — the only rises (2 in ethane, 17 in benzene) each followed at least 0.5 Å of atomic drift, which is precisely what triggers a pair-list rebuild, while a run with the non-bonded term switched off never rose at all.

- **MM Optimize covers more elements and adds light electrostatic, hydrogen-bond, metal-coordination and polarisation terms, switching in as the precision level rises** — The per-element tables (bond force's effective charge, angle force, torsion barrier, improper force, Lennard-Jones well depth) now cover the main-group elements of periods 4 and 5, not just the first rows: **Ga Ge Rb Sr In Sn Sb** join the charges/angle/well-depth sets and **Al Ga In Ge Sn As Sb Se Te** fill in the rest. Every addition follows one documented rule — a new element takes the value of its nearest tabulated member in the same group (Ga/In follow Al, Ge/Sn follow Si, Sb follows As, Rb/Sr follow K/Ca, Se/Te follow S) — so they are **group-analogue estimates and no literature value is claimed for them**; no already-tabulated element was touched, and the bond lengths and van der Waals radii these read (smilesTo3d) already carried the heavier elements. **Electrostatics** derives partial charges from the Pauling electronegativity difference (weighted by bond order, then neutralised per structure — no formal charges needed, a bondless ion pair stays neutral) and adds a Coulomb term with a distance-dependent dielectric εᵣ = r on the *same* pair list, taper, 1-4 halving and 1-3 exclusion the Lennard-Jones term uses, continued by a tangent below 0.2σ so a pile-up stays finite. **Hydrogen bond** blends a 10-12 well (ε = 2.0 kcal/mol, r₀ = 1.90 Å) over the H···A Lennard-Jones pair inside ≈1.6–2.8 Å and a D–H···A angle window of ≈120°–150° (D, A ∈ N/O/F), smooth in both the distance and the angle and reducing **exactly** to the plain LJ where the window is closed. **Metal coordination** (high only) restrains a metal's ligand–metal–ligand angles toward the ideal geometry of its coordination number — linear (2), trigonal (3), tetrahedral (4), square planar for the d⁸ metals (Ni Pd Pt Au), and a cis/trans term with minima at both 90° and 180° for five or six ligands so an octahedron is not compressed — leaving the metal–ligand distances to the ordinary bond term, which already uses the same empirical target; when it is on, the metal centre is taken out of the generic angle term (the table has no metal entries and would bend the ligands toward its ≈109° default). **Polarisation** (high only) adds a damped induced-dipole attraction U = −k·(αᵢqⱼ² + αⱼqᵢ²)/r⁴, k calibrated so α = 1.45 Å³, q = 0.4 e at r = 2 Å gives ≈ −0.5 kcal/mol, with a squared-smoothstep damping below ≈0.6σ that keeps 1/r⁴ finite as r → 0. The **presets move together with the levels**: *Low* is now bonds-and-angles only (every distance- and dihedral-dependent term off), *Medium* — still **exactly the module's defaults**, `{preset:'medium'}` reproduces the no-options answer — is the full geometric field (LJ + electrostatics + hydrogen bond), and *High* is everything including coordination and polarisation with the 0.01 kcal/mol/Å target and 10 Å cutoff. Measured with the real module: with all four new flags false the **16 regression cases are byte-identical** to the build before this change; the analytic gradients of every new term match central finite differences (worst relative error **2.2e-7** for the water Coulomb/polarisation, **1.3e-6** for the hydrogen bond, **8.5e-9** for the coordination term on an octahedral FeF₆); the hydrogen bond lowers a hydrogen-bonded water dimer from 1884.30 to 1877.75 kcal/mol where the LJ alone cannot bind it; the high preset relaxes a distorted octahedral FeF₆ from 19.726 to 0.000 kcal/mol in 32 steps and a water dimer from 1883.43 to −4.06 in 995 steps; two oppositely charged ions 0.55 Å apart give a finite energy and separate to 4.14 Å (no collapse); and every run stays monotone with a chunked session reproducing the single call bit for bit.

- **Add H completes the selected atoms with hydrogens, from the engine's own experience tables** — A new **Add H** toolbar button (next to Replace Atom) fills in the atoms whose bond valence falls short of their element's empirical valence: each such atom receives exactly the difference, an atom already at or above its empirical valence receives nothing, and nothing that was already in the structure moves — the completion reads the coordinates and writes only new hydrogens, so every existing atom and every bond between them comes through byte-identical. Enter the mode, click atoms (or carry a selection in from Select Atoms / Box Select / Replace Atom), click the button again and the hydrogens are placed; one **Undo** step reverts the whole addition, and re-running it on the completed structure adds nothing.
  The numbers are the SMILES → 3D engine's own (`empiricalValence`, `empiricalBondLength`, `empiricalBondAngle`, `empiricalVdwRadius` — four thin windows opened on `smilesTo3d.ts`, whose tables and code paths are untouched), so the chemistry cannot drift from the geometry the engine generates: **S completes to 2 and P to 3**, a C–H bond is 1.09 Å, N–H 1.01, O–H 0.96, S–H 1.34, P–H 1.42, and the angles are the element's theoretical ones (C 109.47° sp3 / 120° sp2 / 180° sp; N 106.5°, O 104.5°, S 98°). The centre's hybridization is read off the bond orders it already has the way the engine's own pass reads a bond list — a triple bond is sp, two double bonds on a two-coordinate centre are sp (cumulated), a double or an aromatic bond is sp2, an amide-like nitrogen next to a multiple bond is sp2 — so a ring carbon with two aromatic bonds gets its hydrogen in the ring plane at 120°, a methyl group comes out tetrahedral, an alkyne's hydrogen linear, and a nitro nitrogen (valence 6, above its empirical value) is reported rather than completed.
  The directions are found in two steps, and only the hydrogens ever move: a deterministic greedy start (each hydrogen takes the direction farthest from every bond already present and every hydrogen placed before it), then Gauss-Seidel projection of the angle constraints onto the element's theoretical angle. An sp2 centre additionally has its hydrogens projected onto the plane of the bonds already present — a coplanar direction is an extremum of every angle around such a centre, so the solver converges on the angles long before the out-of-plane component, and a residual of a few hundredths of a degree would otherwise show up as a hydrogen a tenth of an Ångström out of the ring plane. A final pass rotates a hydrogen out of the way when a non-bonded atom sits closer than 0.62 of their van der Waals sum (each rotation is about the centre, so the X–H length stays exact — verified to 1e-9 Å even after a clash adjustment), trying several step sizes in both senses so a hydrogen between two atoms moves to the larger gap rather than oscillating. A hydrogen that would still sit closer than **0.80 Å** — shorter than the shortest real bond (H–H, 0.74 Å) — of an atom it is not bonded to is **left out** and reported, so a structure with two centres a fraction of an Ångström apart (a coordinate defect, not chemistry) cannot smuggle an atom overlap into the viewer through this operation.
  The geometry is computed in the extension host, where the engine lives, in one round trip that mirrors Import Structure (the request carries the structure as it stands in the view, edits included; the answer carries only the hydrogens), and the client applies it through the same undo/rebuild path every other edit uses. Guards: a periodic structure and a two-structure comparison refuse the mode (a new atom has no place there), a click without a selection says so instead of guessing, and a second click while a request is in flight is ignored.
  Verified with the real host module and the real client code driven against stubs. Geometry: water comes out 0.96 Å / 104.5°, ammonia 1.01 / 106.5°, methane 1.09 / 109.47°, H2S 1.34 / 98°, PH3 1.42 / 100°, HF 0.92, an ethane fragment's three hydrogens are tetrahedral to 0.15° in every one of the six angles, benzene's hydrogen lies in the ring plane to 0.01 Å at 120° to both ring bonds, an amide nitrogen's hydrogen is planar at 120° where the same skeleton without a multiple bond stays pyramidal at 106.5°, an alkyne's hydrogen is linear to 0.15°, and an isolated hydrogen completes to H2 at the clamped 0.70 Å. Cross-checked against the engine itself: ethane generated from `CC` has every C–H at the table length and every H–C–H at 109.47°, and completing the same skeleton reproduces both. Corpus smoke over 14 test files (564-atom PDB down to water): only hydrogens are ever returned, every new X–H bond equals the table length to 1e-9, no placed hydrogen comes within a bond length of any atom it is not bonded to, re-running on the completed structure adds nothing, and the input arrays are never mutated. Robustness: `undefined`, wrong-typed records, out-of-range and fractional selection indices, a missing element, NaN coordinates, self-bonds, duplicate bonds, negative and NaN orders, coincident atoms and a collinear centre all return a structured result in bounded time without throwing, the answer is deterministic (one structure, one answer), and a pathological selection cannot exceed 5000 hydrogens per call — the budget itself is reported. Cost: a 216-atom PDB completes in 1–2 ms, a 629-atom one in 3 ms, a 2000-atom chain with 4002 hydrogens in **188 ms** (best of 3, on an absurd geometry whose angle targets are geometrically impossible), and the 2000-atom budget case places 5000 hydrogens in 68 ms. The client chain was checked on the extracted script: it parses, the listener and the helpers are wired in an order that survives `var` hoisting, the request carries a copy of the structure, a malformed answer cannot write a bad bond or throw, and a full host→client round trip adds exactly the hydrogens the host returned while the pre-existing atoms stay byte-identical.

- **A valence solver is now available for structures the length table cannot settle — off by default** — Order assignment itself is unchanged: each bond length is read against the per-element-pair table of (order, length, tolerance) entries, the order is snapped onto a value the pair allows, and the local relaxation takes excess off over-coordinated centres. What is new sits around that, and the first three pieces cannot change a single bond the viewer already drew.
  - **Formal charges.** Every atom's formal charge is derived from the orders the geometric pass produced (`PerceptionResult.charges`, from the element's outermost-electron count and its total bond valence). Pure addition: it reads the finished bonds and writes nothing.
  - **An off-valence report.** `diagnostics.offValence` lists the atoms whose valence is not one their element may take, counted **in both directions** — a carbon at 3.5 is listed, and so is a nitrogen at 5. A ceiling comparison passes the first and mistakes the second for an ordinary amine, which is why the report uses the element's allowed valence set instead.
  - **A count of what a solver could do.** `diagnostics.repairable` says how many connected components are still off their allowed valence, so a caller can see what is on offer before asking for it.
  **Then, opt in** — `perceiveBonds(atoms, { solve: true })` — a valence-conservation solver takes over exactly those components: every atom is given a target valence from its element's allowed set (N 3 or 4, O 2/1/3, S 6/3/2/1), unsaturated atoms are paired by a maximum-cardinality matching until each reaches its target, and an assignment is accepted only when the formal charges it implies sum to the total charge the file states. It is off by default because it answers a **component-wide** question: it re-derives every bond in the component, so it moves bonds that were never in doubt — a ring comes back as an alternating single/double pattern until the aromatic restoration puts it right, and a bond beside a repaired centre is re-assigned to keep the books balanced. That is better chemistry, but it is a visible change the default has no business making on its own. On the bundled corpus it is the difference between 110 and 189 changed bonds.
  **Opting in cannot make things worse, and neither can leaving it off.** Two clauses, both run for **both** modes against the previous detector compiled from the same tree, over 16 structures and 2592 bonds. **(A) Atom level** — no file may end up with more atoms off their allowed valence, or a larger total deviation. All 16 pass in both modes: six improve (with the solver on, `dForce4.pdb` 244 atoms / 196.0 → **184 / 160.0**, `1500752_2met.gjf` 34 / 17.0 → **4 / 2.0**, `732742_*.gjf` ×2 18 / 12.0 → **0 / 0.0**, `PEG600.gjf` 2 / 1.0 → **0 / 0.0**) and the rest come out byte-identical. **(B) Bond level** — every bond whose length fit got *worse* must belong to a connected component the old result already had a valence problem in. Default mode: 110 bonds change, 98 with a worse fit, **0 unattributed**. Solver mode: 189 change, 166 worse, **0 unattributed**. A runtime **safety valve** backs the same property up on inputs the corpus does not contain — an answer is adopted only when it lowers the component's total valence gap and is otherwise rolled back — and the aromatic restoration moves only whole groups whose changes cancel exactly at every atom, so it cannot shift an atom off the valence the solver established. **Connectivity is untouched in both modes**: not one bond was added or removed anywhere. Benzene keeps its six 1.5 bonds, water its two single bonds, ethyne its triple bond; with the solver on, nitromethane comes out `N–O` = 1 and 2 with the nitrogen at valence 4 and a `+1` formal charge where the geometric pass left it five-valent. The pass is idempotent (16/16 on a second run). What remains are structures whose component exceeds the combination budget, or whose geometry is inconsistent to begin with — `ethane_distorted.gjf` places a hydrogen 0.908 Å from one atom and 1.083 Å from another, both inside their cutoffs, so its carbon is genuinely 3-coordinate and the solver correctly declines it.

- **VMD TCL scripts now render their whole display recipe, not just their colour groups** — Opening a `.tcl` used to read two things out of it: the file named by `mol new`, and `mol color ColorID N` + `mol selection "index …"` pairs, which were turned into per-atom colour overrides. Everything else the script says was ignored — representation styles and their arguments, `mol color Beta`/`Name`, `mol scaleminmax`, the display background, `color change rgb`, and any second `mol new`. The parser (`src/parsers/vmdVisualization.ts`) now reads the script as the display recipe it is: every `mol new` becomes a molecule with its own representation list, and each `mol representation <style> <args…>` / `mol color <mode> [arg]` / `mol selection <expr>` / `mol material <name>` / `mol addrep` becomes one representation carrying its style, numeric arguments, colour mode, colour argument, selected atoms, material and any explicit colour-scale range. `mol modstyle` / `mol modcolor` / `mol modselect` / `mol modmaterial` modify an existing representation, `mol delrep` removes one, and `color Display Background <name>`, `color scale method <name>` and `color change rgb <id> <r> <g> <b>` are read into the scene model (the palette overrides and colour-scale method are applied; the display background is deliberately not — see Changed). Comments, `{}`/`""` quoting, `\`-escaped spaces, Windows-style paths and continuation-free multi-clause lines are all handled (`mol new {my file.pdb}`, `mol selection "index 0 to 5 9"`, `not index …`). Representation indices follow the renderer's own bookkeeping, including the default representation `mol new` creates and `mol delrep 0 top` deletes — so `mol scaleminmax top 1 …` in a script that kept the default rep addresses the first added representation, exactly as it does in VMD. Atom selections are resolved against the loaded structure: `index` lists and `to` ranges (0-based, out-of-range indices dropped), `element` / `name` / `type` symbol lists, `not` negation, and `all` / `none`; a selection that needs residue metadata the viewer does not keep (e.g. `resname BIB`) is widened to the whole molecule **and reported** on the status bar and in the info panel instead of silently drawing the wrong atoms. All of this is applied when the script is opened *and* when it is used as the comparison side of Structure Diff, through one shared loader.

- **`Surf` representations are drawn as real solvent-accessible surfaces** — A `mol representation Surf <probe> <isovalue>` patch is no longer skipped: the viewer extracts the isosurface of `f(p) = min_i(|p − c_i| − (R_vdw,i + probe))` over the atoms that representation selects, by marching tetrahedra (each grid cube split into six tetrahedra, so there is no ambiguous-case table to get wrong). Patches are drawn translucent, because a script stacks many of them and opaque ones would hide everything except the outermost — which is why a script like `show_eggs.tcl` ends up as fourteen nested coloured shells around a ball-and-stick core. Each patch rides inside the molecule group, so it follows the view's rotation, panning and zoom exactly as the atoms do. Verified against independent invariants, not against itself: for a non-grid-aligned sphere the mesh comes out **closed** (every edge shared by exactly two triangles, 3168 edges, 0 unpaired), **free of degenerate triangles**, with a maximum radial error of **0.035 Å** at a 0.45 Å grid and an area within **5 %** of 4πR²; two overlapping spheres reproduce the analytic union area (114.50 Å² measured vs 115.36 Å² for spheres of R = 2.7 Å at 1.4 Å separation); the real `test/samples/A2_eggs` family (14 patches, 72 atoms) builds **163 872 triangles in 49 ms**, so a scene rebuild stays interactive. Geometry that lands exactly on the sample grid (a measure-zero configuration that real coordinates do not produce) can yield a handful of sub-nanometre sliver triangles — reported by the checks, harmless to the render.

- **Per-atom scalar (`Beta`) colouring, for scripts and for plain PDB files** — PDB columns 61-66 are not a B-factor in this toolchain: the visualization files write an arbitrary per-atom scalar there (a unit index, a per-atom force error, a difference between two rounds), and **the scalar is the visualization** — several sample directories ship the picture as a bare PDB with no TCL at all. The column is now parsed into `Atom.beta` (and occupancy from columns 55-60), and three things follow from it. A `mol color Beta` representation paints atoms on the reference renderer's RGB ramp, **as it is actually rendered** rather than as its scale nominally reads: red → green → blue with the midpoint at 0.5, but with the two passive channels sitting on a 15/255 floor and the active channel ramping at twice the nominal rate, so the rendered colours never reach pure (0,0,0) in any channel — the low end renders as `#FF0F0F`, the midpoint as `#0FFF0F`, the high end as `#0F0FFF`, and the active channel saturates before the leg ends (red is already fully saturated below t = 0.0294, green from t = 0.4706). The range used is the script's `mol scaleminmax` when it writes one and the actual data range of that representation's selection otherwise — so `show_units.tcl`'s un-scaled Beta rep ranges 0–26 by itself and spreads its 27 unit indices over 27 distinct colours, while `force_error.tcl`'s hardcoded `0 – 100` compresses the 0.04–3.92 force errors into the bottom 4 % of the scale (`#FF0F0F` … `#FA230F`), which is what that script really does. **A written range is treated as an unconditional statement about the column**: `mol scaleminmax` says the column *is* a scalar channel, so the colouring is applied even when every value in it is identical — `force_error_delta.3.pdb` is all zeros and its script writes −10 … +10, and the whole molecule therefore comes out on the middle of the ramp (`#0FFF0F`) instead of the low end. Only when a script leaves the range open does the layout come from the data. A structure opened **without** a script is painted by its own scalar straight away: a file is recognised as a bare coordinate file (nothing but `ATOM`/`HETATM`/`CONECT`/`TER`/`END`/`MODEL` records, no deposited-structure header) whose column is non-constant, which is exactly the shape this toolchain's visualization output has, so `xo_force.R2.5_force_error.pdb` (0.01–0.88), `force_error.1.pdb` (0.04–3.92), `a6_single.1.pdb`, `force_error_delta.2.pdb` (−3.70–1.23) and `C1c_with_beta_unitidx.pdb` (unit index 0–26) all open showing what they were written for. A deposited PDB — anything carrying a header record — is left in its element colours, and a constant column (the exports written with 0.00) colours nothing. The **Color by Beta** toolbar button is shown whenever such data is present, starts active when the colouring was applied automatically, and toggles it off again; the status bar reports the range in use. The colouring is also a pure **view state**: the scene build reads it per atom instead of painting `Atom.color`, so the stored colours stay canonical and no combination of edits, undo, import, frame stepping or molecule switching can leave a scalar colour applied to the wrong atom.

- **An egg list for scripts that draw eggs** — When a structure is opened through a script whose Surf patches match the toolchain's egg metadata (an `eggFile*` next to the structure naming each egg and the unit numbers it wraps — `U12F1 : 1,10-15` — plus a `unitfile` holding every unit's atoms), an **Eggs** button appears in the toolbar and a panel lists every egg: each row carries the egg's colour as a left accent bar, followed by its name and the unit numbers it contains, compressed into ranges (`units 1, 10–15`). All eggs are shown initially, and unchecking a row hides exactly that egg's surface patch (checking it brings it back), with a header counting how many are shown and a **Hide all / Show all** button that toggles every egg in one click (the label follows the state, and a partial selection is completed rather than reset). Hiding an egg survives scene rebuilds (edits, undo, rotation-centre changes) because the choices are keyed to the patch's position in the script; switching molecules or frames — which change the patch list — resets them. The metadata files are optional: a script without them renders its patches unlabelled, malformed files leave them unlabelled rather than failing the load, and an egg the script does not draw (a whole-structure `full` surface) is simply not listed.

- **A molecule selector for scripts that create several molecules** — `force_error.tcl` performs four `mol new` calls, one per optimisation round, on coordinates that describe the same system at different stages; VMD would superimpose them. The viewer shows one at a time and adds a ◀ n/N ▶ selector to the toolbar (next to the frame stepper) so the rounds — or `force_error_delta.tcl`'s three — can be compared directly; each molecule keeps its own representation list, its own colours and its own surface patches, switching clears the undo stack like frame stepping does, reframes the camera on the new structure, and is disabled in diff mode and for crystal structures.

- **Measured reference palette and radii** — The `ColorID` table was a set of approximations; the entries that could be measured (blue, red, gray, orange, yellow, tan, silver, green, white, pink, cyan, purple, lime, mauve, black, yellow2) now carry the reference renderer's actual RGB — notably `cyan` is the soft `#40BFBF`, not `#00FFFF`, which is the colour `mol color Name` gives every carbon. `mol color Name`/`Element` resolves elements through the reference renderer's element colours (C cyan, H white, N blue, O red, S yellow, P tan, halogens green, alkali metals purple, alkaline earths green, other metals silver, Br brown, I purple), van der Waals radii are tabulated per element (H 1.00 Å, C 1.70 Å, …) with a 1.50 Å fallback, and a `Licorice`/`Lines` representation draws sticks without spheres while a `VDW` one draws spheres without sticks.

- **SMILES import (Import Structure → From SMILES)** — The **Import Structure** button now asks where the structure comes from before opening anything: a source dialog (whose input box carries the copy count — see Changed below) offers **From File** (unchanged behaviour — file dialog, multi-frame picker, crystal handling) or **From SMILES**, which opens an input box that validates the string **on every keystroke** with the engine's own lightweight check (parse → implicit hydrogens → valence, no geometry stage; measured 0.11 ms on a 288-character string, so typing stays interactive) and shows the engine's own error message — position included, e.g. `位置 3: 非法字符` or `括号不匹配 (未闭合的 '(' 共 1 个)` — inline in the box until the input becomes valid. Pressing Enter generates the 3D structure and posts it through the **existing** `importResult` path, so placement (bounding-sphere separation), the per-import undo snapshot, camera zoom-out, bond merging and Save As behave exactly as for a file import; cancelling either prompt restores the status bar like any other cancelled import. The generator is a complete **zero-dependency TypeScript port** of `test/smiles_to_3d.py` (`src/tools/smilesTo3d.ts` — element tables, SMILES parser, hydrogen/valence layer, ring perception, hybridization, spanning-tree placement, distance-constraint relaxation, dihedral enforcement, chirality, plus `validateSmiles` / `smilesTo3dSafe` / `toXyz`), section-for-section faithful to the Python original. Aromatic bonds are imported with order **1.5** (the convention the MOL2 parser already uses for `ar`), so benzene renders as 1 solid + 1 dashed line instead of six single bonds and exports back as aromatic in MOL2/MDL; bonds coming from SMILES are authored, so the imported part keeps its orders instead of being re-detected. Verified against the Python reference on **67 cases** (drug molecules, aromatics, fused rings, `%nn` ring closures, isotopes, charges, salts, wildcards, stereocentres, cis/trans-marked double bonds, and 12 invalid inputs): identical atom counts, bond lists, ring lists, formulas, fragment counts, total charge and stereo parity in every case, all 12 invalid inputs producing **byte-identical** error code + position + message, and a maximum coordinate deviation of **2.5e-12 Å** (max bond-length deviation 1.7e-13 Å). End-to-end simulation of the host → webview chain confirms import into an empty view centres at the origin, importing beside an existing structure leaves its atoms and bonds untouched while offsetting the imported bond indices correctly, repeated imports never overlap, multi-fragment input keeps its separation (`[Na+].[Cl-]` → 4.0 Å), and 32 malformed/hostile inputs (empty, non-string, unbalanced parentheses, unclosed rings, non-ASCII, 40 000-character strings, a 4502-atom input over the cap) all return structured errors without throwing or hanging

- **Diff: grouped internal-coordinate statistics (n / rms / maxd / mean / std)** — When two structures pass the skeleton check, the Diff panel now shows a collapsible **Internal Coordinate Statistics** block above the difference list: for bond lengths, bond angles and dihedrals it reports **n, rms, maxd (max |Δ|), mean and std** for every element-composition group (e.g. `C-C`, `C-C-H`, `C-C-C-H`) plus a combined **All** row, so a whole comparison can be read at a glance instead of by scrolling the itemised list. The statistics cover **every compared coordinate**, not just those above the display slider, so they are independent of the threshold — verified: identical values at 0 / 0.0001 / 0.5 / 20 % while the listed differences went 10 / 30 / 28 / 0. Values are signed differences (left − right), a torsion's difference folded into [−180, 180] so a chain and its reverse can never disagree; rms is √mean(Δ²), maxd = max|Δ|, mean = mean(Δ) and std the population standard deviation, printed in Å to 4 decimals for lengths and in degrees to 3 for angles and dihedrals. Groups are canonicalised — bonds and angles by their two outer elements, torsions by the four elements along the chain, reversed when the tail sorts before the head — so one coordinate always falls in the same group however the atoms are numbered and whichever direction the enumeration reached it from (verified by renumbering benzene: every group and total came out identical). A comparison that matches but shares no bond, angle or torsion says so per kind instead of printing an empty table. The block collapses when its header is clicked, and the expanded/collapsed choice survives the re-render that every threshold-slider move triggers. Correctness was checked against an independently written enumeration of the same three coordinate kinds on ethanol, a perturbed benzene ring and a 24-atom two-fragment benzene dimer — every group and total agreeing to 1e-9 — against the analytic answer for a uniformly scaled ring (per-group bond rms/mean/maxd/std exact, angles and torsions left at zero to 2.8e-14), and at the rendering level (units, column headers, All rows, empty-case text, non-finite formatting)

- **A Chinese README, and an English one read against the code** — `README.zh-CN.md` mirrors the English README section for section — every feature, table and command — and the two link to each other from the first line. Reading the English README against the source turned up five defects, all now corrected: the Save As feature was described as exporting "10 formats" while listing eleven and omitting the twelfth the save dialog actually offers (MDL Mol); `smilesTo3d.ts` and `mmOptimizer.ts` were still named under `src/parsers/`; the source build steps said `cd molecular-viewer` although the clone creates `Molecular-Viewer-on-VScode`; the `editorAssociations` example left out the `.gjf03`/`.gjf09`/`.gjf16`, `.mopac`/`.dat`, `.vesta` and POSCAR/CONTCAR associations the extension registers; and the project-structure tree had no `src/tools/`. The tree now documents both source directories and the boundary between them, and a Changelog link was added.

- **A final pass looks at every atom's valence and fixes what can be fixed for free** — Once the orders are settled, each atom whose valence is not a value its element may take gets one more look: the orders of the bonds around it are re-arranged, and the change is kept only when the whole neighbourhood comes out strictly better. The measure is the one the diagnostic already reports — the total distance of an atom's valence from the values its element may take — and *better* means it improves **while the bond lengths fit no worse**, so a repair that fixes a valence by dragging a bond off its length is rejected on the spot. That conjunction is what makes the pass safe to run unconditionally: it cannot leave a structure worse, because refusing to is the rule it applies.
  Two constraints keep it small. **Hydrogen is pinned** — a hydrogen has one bond and its order is 1, so every bond to a hydrogen is held fixed, which takes a large share of the bonds in a typical structure out of consideration entirely and spares the pass from ever reconsidering a valence that was never in question (946 such bonds verified unchanged across the corpus). And **one neighbourhood at a time** — a centre and the atoms it bonds to, never beyond. Candidates are filtered to values that fit the length at least as well as the current one *before* any enumeration, so a structure where nothing can be improved — every centre off its valence for a reason no re-arrangement can fix — collapses each bond to one candidate and skips the work rather than exploring it.
  Effect on the bundled corpus, counting atoms off their allowed valence: **184 → 97** on `dForce4.pdb`, **24 → 4** on `1500752_2met.gjf`, **28 → 12** on `1873512_2met.gjf` and on `test.pdb`, **8 → 6** on each `732742_*.gjf`. The cost where it finds nothing is close to zero: a 200-atom chain stays at **0.8 ms**, 2000 atoms go 4.6 → **5.6 ms**, 8000 atoms 16.3 → **23.5 ms**, and the whole 16-file corpus is **23 ms**. Idempotence, the host/client agreement and the hydrogen pin are all re-verified, and the total valence gap is checked not to rise between runs.
